### [1] [A New Error Analysis for a Point Tracking Elliptic Distributed Optimal Control Problem with Pointwise Control Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04281)
*Susanne C. Brenner,Li-yeng Sung*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper develops a unified error analysis framework for a linear-quadratic elliptic distributed optimal control problem with point tracking and pointwise control constraints, covering both standard finite element discretization and variational discretization in a single self-contained analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a concise and unified error analysis for optimal control problems with point tracking and control constraints, which bridges the gap between standard and variational discretization approaches while remaining self-contained for practitioners.

Method: The authors treat standard finite element discretization and variational discretization within a unified framework, building the error analysis on standard finite element techniques and classical results for elliptic partial differential equations, making the analysis self-contained.

Result: A concise and unified error analysis is developed that covers both discretization approaches for the linear-quadratic elliptic distributed optimal control problem with point tracking and pointwise control constraints.

Conclusion: The unified framework allows both standard and variational discretizations to be analyzed together, with the error analysis resting on well-established finite element and PDE tools rather than problem-specific arguments.

Abstract: We develop a concise error analysis for a linear-quadratic elliptic distributed optimal control problem with point tracking and pointwise control constraints, where a standard finite element discretization and a variational discretization are treated in a unified framework. The analysis is self-contained modulo standard techniques in finite element analysis and classical results for elliptic partial differential equations.

</details>


### [2] [A Weighted Upwind Vector Kinetic Lattice Boltzmann Method For Hyperbolic Conservation Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04283)
*Michael W. Brown,Jehanzeb Chaudhry,John N. Shadid*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper introduces novel upwinded equilibrium distribution functions for Vector kinetic lattice Boltzmann (VKLB) methods, enabling their application to general hyperbolic PDE systems. The method uses continuous flux vector splitting based on eigendecomposition of the flux Jacobian and is verified on shallow water, Euler, and ideal MHD equations, showing improved stability, reduced error norms, and sharper shock resolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VKLB methods have emerged as a promising framework for solving hyperbolic PDE systems, but their applicability to general hyperbolic systems has been limited. The authors aim to broaden the applicability of VKLB methods by constructing novel upwinded equilibrium distribution functions that work for general hyperbolic systems, not just specific cases.

Method: The authors construct new upwinded equilibrium distribution functions from conservation variables and numerical fluxes derived via continuous flux vector splitting, which is based on the eigendecomposition of the flux Jacobian. These upwinded equilibria are incorporated into the VKLB framework with discrete moment constraints and carefully defined equilibrium distributions, creating the weighted-upwind VKLB formulation for general hyperbolic systems.

Result: The method is verified on challenging hyperbolic systems including shallow water, Euler, and ideal MHD equations. Results demonstrate improved stability, reduced error norms, and sharper shock resolution across increasingly complex verification and benchmark problems.

Conclusion: The weighted-upwind VKLB method with eigendecomposition-based flux vector splitting provides a broadly applicable, stable, and accurate framework for solving general hyperbolic PDE systems, outperforming prior approaches in stability and accuracy across multiple benchmark problems.

Abstract: Vector kinetic lattice Boltzmann (VKLB) methods have recently emerged as a promising framework for solving hyperbolic partial differential equation (PDE) systems. VKLB discretizes Boltzmann-type equations using a discrete set of lattice velocities, enforces discrete moment constraints, and carefully defines equilibrium distribution functions. In this work, we introduce novel upwinded equilibrium distribution functions constructed from conservation variables and numerical fluxes derived via continuous flux vector splitting based on the eigendecomposition of the flux Jacobian. This formulation enables the weighted-upwind VKLB equilibrium to be applied broadly to general hyperbolic systems. The method is verified on a set of challenging hyperbolic systems that includes the shallow water, Euler and ideal magnetohydrodynamics (MHD) equations. The proposed method demonstrates improved stability, reduced error norms, and sharper shock resolution across increasingly complex verification and benchmark problems.

</details>


### [3] [Error estimates for vector field interpolation based on generalized matrix-valued kernels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04313)
*Zhengjie Sun,Lishuo Dong,Leevan Ling*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper develops an operator-based framework for constructing divergence-free and curl-free matrix-valued kernels using integral and differential operators, which relaxes regularity requirements compared to classical potential-based approaches. The authors prove norm-equivalence of native spaces to vector-valued Sobolev spaces via dimension-walking, derive sharp direct Sobolev error estimates with fractional regularity, establish Bernstein-type inequalities leading to a complete inverse theorem, and prove eigenvalue lower bounds for stability. Numerical experiments verify the theory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical potential-based constructions of divergence-free and curl-free matrix-valued kernels enforce structural constraints naturally but require the generating scalar function to have relatively high smoothness. This restricts their practical applicability when only lower-regularity data or target fields are available.

Method: The authors introduce an operator-based framework using integral and differential operators to construct divergence-free and curl-free matrix-valued kernels, relaxing regularity requirements. They employ dimension-walking techniques to establish norm-equivalence to Sobolev spaces, derive direct Sobolev error estimates supporting fractional regularity, establish Bernstein-type inequalities, and analyze the smallest eigenvalues of interpolation matrices for stability.

Result: The framework substantially relaxes regularity requirements of potential-based approaches while preserving structural constraints. The native spaces are norm-equivalent to vector-valued Sobolev spaces, sharp error estimates with fractional regularity are obtained, Bernstein-type inequalities yield a complete inverse theorem, and eigenvalue lower bounds confirm stability. Numerical experiments verify all theoretical findings.

Conclusion: The operator-based construction provides a more flexible and general framework for matrix-valued kernel interpolation with reduced smoothness requirements, supported by a complete theoretical analysis covering approximation, inverse, and stability results validated numerically.

Abstract: Matrix-valued kernels provide a flexible framework for approximating vector fields from scattered data, especially when structural constraints such as divergence-free or curl-free conditions must be preserved. Classical potential-based constructions enforce these constraints naturally, but they typically require the generating scalar function to possess relatively high smoothness. We develop an operator-based framework for constructing div-free and curl-free matrix-valued kernels using integral and differential operators, which substantially relaxes the regularity requirements of the potential approach. Using dimension-walking techniques, we show that the resulting native spaces are norm-equivalent to appropriate vector-valued Sobolev spaces. Another main contribution of the paper is a sharp error analysis for the corresponding kernel matrix-valued interpolation problem. We derive direct Sobolev error estimates that allow fractional regularity of the target field, and we establish Bernstein-type inequalities for the associated kernel trial spaces. These results lead to a complete inverse theorem. We also investigate stability by proving lower bounds for the smallest eigenvalues of the interpolation matrices. Numerical experiments are included to verify the theoretical results.

</details>


### [4] [The Refined Joint Bidiagonalization Method and an Implicitly Restarted Algorithm for Large GSVD Computations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04360)
*Kaixiao Fang,Zhongxiao Jia*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the convergence behavior of the joint bidiagonalization (JBD) method for computing extreme GSVD components, showing that Ritz vectors may converge erratically or even fail to converge while Ritz values converge. The authors prove interlacing properties of Ritz values and generalized singular values, then propose a refined JBD (RJBD) method that ensures unconditional convergence of both left and right refined Ritz vectors under the same hypotheses. They also develop an implicitly restarted RJBD algorithm with refined shifts, which demonstrates competitive or superior efficiency compared to the standard implicitly restarted JBD algorithm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The JBD method is widely used for computing GSVD components, but its convergence behavior (especially for Ritz vectors) is not well understood. Understanding and overcoming potential erratic or non-convergent behavior of Ritz vectors is important for the reliability and practical utility of the method.

Method: The authors (1) formally analyze convergence of JBD, showing Ritz values converge but Ritz vectors may not; (2) prove interlacing properties between Ritz values and generalized singular values; (3) extend the refined Rayleigh-Ritz projection from eigenvalue problems to GSVD, defining right and left refined Ritz vectors; (4) prove unconditional convergence of refined Ritz vectors; (5) extend implicit restarting to the refined JBD method with proposed refined shifts.

Result: Theoretical results show that while Ritz values from JBD converge, Ritz vectors may converge erratically or fail to converge. The proposed RJBD method's refined Ritz vectors provably converge unconditionally. Numerical experiments demonstrate that the implicitly restarted RJBD algorithm is at least competitive with, and often considerably more efficient than, the implicitly restarted JBD algorithm.

Conclusion: The refined JBD method successfully addresses the convergence issues of standard JBD by guaranteeing unconditional convergence of Ritz vectors, and the implicitly restarted RJBD algorithm offers improved practical efficiency.

Abstract: We make a convergence analysis on the joint bidiagonalization (JBD) method that computes several extreme generalized singular value decomposition (GSVD) components of a regular matrix pair $\{A,L\}$, and show that the right and left Ritz vectors obtained by it may converge erratically and even may fail to converge, while Ritz values converge. These convergence results hold for a class of general Rayleigh--Ritz projection methods for the GSVD problem under the hypothesis that the deviation of a desired right generalized singular vector from the right subspace tends to zero. We prove the interlacing property of Ritz values and generalized singular values, and extend it to the generalized singular values of $\{A,L\}$ and the matrix pairs consisting of subsets of its columns. To overcome the irregular convergence or possible non-convergence of the JBD method, we nontrivially extend the refined Rayleigh--Ritz projection for the eigenvalue problem to the GSVD problem, and propose a refined JBD (RJBD) method that replaces the right Ritz vectors by new approximations, called the right refined Ritz vectors, satisfying certain residual optimality; we define new approximate left generalized singular vectors, called the left refined Ritz vectors. We prove that the left and right refined Ritz vectors unconditionally converge under the same hypothesis. We extend the implicit restarting scheme to the RJBD method, and develop an implicitly restarted RJBD algorithm with the refined shifts proposed. Numerical experiments illustrate that the new algorithm is at least competitive and often considerably more efficient than the implicitly restarted JBD algorithm.

</details>


### [5] [An adaptive split-combine Gaussian mixture filter for nonlinear and multimodal state estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04430)
*San Kim,Won Chang,Daniel B. Forger,Dae Wook Kim*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper introduces an adaptive split-combine Gaussian mixture filter (AMF) that estimates time-evolving asymmetric and multimodal probability density functions in nonlinear systems by adaptively splitting and combining Gaussian particles without auxiliary online numerical optimization. The method guarantees variance reduction in splitting and outperforms baseline filters on benchmarks including Van der Pol oscillators and the Lorenz attractor, with a parallel implementation for practical computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Kalman-type filters rely on single-Gaussian assumptions that fail for nonlinear systems with oscillatory or chaotic dynamics, where PDFs become highly asymmetric and multimodal. Gaussian mixture filtering addresses this, but accurately propagating mixture components and adaptively adjusting their number and weights over time remain open challenges.

Method: The proposed AMF adaptively splits and combines Gaussian particles to estimate the evolution of non-Gaussian PDFs. The splitting method guarantees variance reduction along a target level-set-point direction of a Gaussian particle, enabling accurate and efficient particle propagation without auxiliary online numerical optimization. A parallel implementation is also proposed.

Result: AMF consistently outperforms various baseline filters across diverse benchmarks, including single and coupled slow-fast Van der Pol oscillators and the Lorenz attractor. The parallel implementation enables high-fidelity PDF estimation with practical computational cost.

Conclusion: The adaptive split-combine Gaussian mixture filter provides an accurate and computationally practical approach for estimating asymmetric and multimodal PDFs in nonlinear dynamical systems, overcoming limitations of existing Gaussian mixture filtering approaches.

Abstract: Filtering combines model predictions with measurements to estimate the probability density function (PDF) of a system state over time. The PDF often becomes highly asymmetric and even multimodal in nonlinear systems with oscillatory or chaotic dynamics. Such non-Gaussian features violate the single-Gaussian assumption underlying Kalman-type filters. To address this problem, Gaussian mixture filtering has been proposed. However, accurately propagating mixture components and adaptively adjusting their number and weights over time remain open challenges. Here, we develop an adaptive split-combine Gaussian mixture filter (AMF) that estimates the time evolution of asymmetric and multimodal PDFs by adaptively splitting and combining Gaussian particles without auxiliary online numerical optimization. Notably, the proposed splitting method guarantees a reduction in variance along a target level-set-point direction of a Gaussian particle. This enables accurate and efficient propagation of particles. We show that AMF consistently outperforms various baseline filters across diverse benchmarks, including single and coupled slow-fast Van der Pol oscillators and the Lorenz attractor. We also propose a parallel implementation of AMF, allowing high-fidelity PDF estimation with practical computational cost.

</details>


### [6] [An unfitted finite element discrete fracture model for low-permeability barriers via local stiffness matrix modification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04431)
*Ziyao Xu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper proposes a simple extension of the linear finite element method to model low-permeability barriers in porous media flows by modifying the local stiffness matrix of barrier-cut elements in closed form, without changing the H^1-conforming P^1 finite element space or requiring mesh fitting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finite element methods and discrete fracture models are well-established for highly conductive fractures, but low-permeability barriers induce pressure discontinuities that continuous elements cannot represent directly, making them difficult to model with standard finite element frameworks.

Method: The method extends the linear finite element method by applying a closed-form modification to the local stiffness matrix of each element cut by a barrier. It retains the same H^1-conforming P^1 finite element space, preserves sparsity and symmetric positive definiteness, requires no mesh fitting, and adds an inexpensive local post-processing step to recover discontinuous pressure fields with sharp jumps at barrier interfaces.

Result: Convergence studies with manufactured solutions and 2D/3D benchmark problems from the literature confirm the effectiveness of the method, including accurate recovery of sharp pressure jumps without smearing.

Conclusion: The proposed method successfully extends standard finite element methodology to handle low-permeability barriers while preserving desirable properties (sparsity, SPD, mesh independence), and provides an efficient post-processing route to recover discontinuous pressure solutions.

Abstract: Finite element methods are among the most widely used discretizations for flow in porous media, and their discrete fracture models (DFMs) for highly conductive fractures are well-established. Low-permeability barriers, by contrast, have long resisted this framework because they induce pressure discontinuities that continuous elements cannot represent directly. In this paper, we propose a simple extension of the linear finite element method for modeling low-permeability barriers through a closed-form modification of the local stiffness matrix of each barrier-cut element. The method retains exactly the same $H^1$-conforming $P^1$-finite element space, preserves the original sparsity pattern and symmetric positive definiteness, requires no mesh fitting, and coincides with the standard finite element method away from barriers. Moreover, an inexpensive, purely local post-processing step recovers the discontinuous pressure field with a sharp jump at the barrier interface, thereby removing the one-element-wide smearing present in the continuous solution. Convergence studies with manufactured solutions and two- and three-dimensional benchmark problems from the literature confirm the effectiveness of the method.

</details>


### [7] [Optimal convergence of adaptive BEM driven by functional-type error estimators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04556)
*Maximilian Brunner,Alexander Freiszlinger,Dirk Pauly,Dirk Praetorius*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper derives functional upper bounds for the potential error in boundary element discretizations of the Laplace-Dirichlet problem, based on local auxiliary problems over boundary vertex patches. The resulting a posteriori error estimator is locally equivalent to the residual error estimator, enabling proofs of R-linear convergence and optimal-rate convergence (in both potential error and estimator) with an adaptive mesh-refining strategy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To obtain reliable functional error bounds for boundary element methods solving the Laplace-Dirichlet problem, and to connect these bounds to the established residual-based error estimation framework so that convergence and optimality can be rigorously established for adaptive algorithms.

Method: Derives functional upper bounds using local auxiliary problems on patches of boundary vertices for the Laplace-Dirichlet boundary element discretization. The resulting estimator is shown to be locally equivalent to the residual error estimator, which is then leveraged to prove convergence and optimality under an adaptive mesh-refinement strategy.

Result: The functional a posteriori error estimator is locally equivalent to the residual estimator. This equivalence yields R-linear convergence of the functional estimator, and both the potential error and the estimator converge at optimal rates with respect to the number of boundary elements under adaptive refinement. Numerical experiments confirm the theory and demonstrate practical performance.

Conclusion: The proposed functional error estimator provides a theoretically sound and practically effective basis for adaptive boundary element solution of the Laplace-Dirichlet problem, achieving optimal convergence rates.

Abstract: In the present work, we derive functional upper bounds for the potential error arising from boundary element discretizations of the Laplace-Dirichlet problem. These bounds are based on local auxiliary problems on patches of boundary vertices and the resulting a posteriori error estimator is shown to be locally equivalent to the well-studied residual error estimator. This equivalence result allows us to prove R-linear convergence of the functional a posteriori error estimator and, together with a suitable mesh-refining strategy, to establish that the potential error as well as the functional error estimator converge with optimal rates with respect to the number of boundary elements. Numerical experiments affirm the theoretical findings and illustrate the practical performance of the related adaptive algorithm driven by the proposed functional error estimator.

</details>


### [8] [A commutant gate for spectral fitting through symmetry forced degeneracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04903)
*Stelios Savva*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Learned spectral models fail in symmetry-forced degenerate sectors due to undefined per-level observables at exact degeneracies and singular eigenvector gradients near crossings. The authors propose a gated estimator that reads symmetry structure directly from observed operators as the linear commutant (via simultaneous block diagonalization and SVD nullspace), switching between projector-trace objectives for forced blocks and per-level targets elsewhere. This achieves machine-precision fitting in both symmetric and symmetry-breaking regimes, with correct classification to noise epsilon ~0.3 where energy clustering fails at 0.02.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learned spectral models suffer fundamental failures at symmetry-forced degenerate sectors: per-level observables are ill-defined when levels coincide exactly, and eigenvector observable gradients carry a singular 1/(λi-λj) factor near symmetry-protected crossings. Standard fixes (regularizing divergence or thresholding gaps) cannot simultaneously protect forced multiplets and distinguish genuinely distinct levels with a fixed gap.

Method: The method gates the fitting process: (1) a gate reads symmetry structure directly from observed operators as the linear commutant of the operator family (via SVD nullspace following Maehara and Murota's simultaneous block diagonalization); (2) block identity is read from the centre of the commutant rather than eigenvalue clustering, making forced-vs-accidental distinctions structural rather than metric; (3) the objective switches between a projector-trace through forced blocks and per-level targets elsewhere.

Result: Under operator estimation noise, the gate classifies correctly to epsilon ~0.3 where energy clustering fails at 0.02. Gated fitting reaches machine-precision truth in both symmetric and symmetry-breaking regimes—the latter converging to a truth that is a singularity of the ungated objective. Observable bias sits at the noise floor. Validation off the regular representation eliminated two defective estimators that all regular representation tests had passed.

Conclusion: The demonstrated gated estimator successfully resolves symmetry-forced degeneracy problems in learned spectral models. A full parametric matrix model with learned matrices is proposed as the next step.

Abstract: Learned spectral models fail at symmetry forced degenerate sectors for two distinct reasons. Where symmetry forces levels to coincide exactly, the per level observable one would normally fit is not well defined, since every unit vector of that shared space is an eigenvector; and near a symmetry protected crossing, the eigenvector observable gradient carries a factor 1/(lambda_i - lambda_j) that is genuinely singular as the gap closes. The usual response is to regularize the divergence or threshold the gap, and both carry a real cost: a fixed gap cannot both protect a forced multiplet and keep two genuinely distinct levels apart. We show a different fix, on synthetic operator families with a known symmetry answer key. A gate reads the symmetry structure directly from the observed operators, as the linear commutant of the family, one singular value decomposition nullspace, following the simultaneous block diagonalization of Maehara and Murota. Block identity is read from the centre of that commutant rather than from eigenvalue clustering, which makes the forced versus accidental distinction structural rather than metric, and the objective switches between a projector trace through forced blocks and a per level target elsewhere. Under operator estimation noise the gate classifies correctly to epsilon of about 0.3, where energy clustering already fails by 0.02. Gated fitting reaches the truth at machine precision in both the symmetric and the symmetry breaking regime, in the latter converging to a truth that is a singularity of the ungated objective, and observable bias sits at the noise floor. Validation off the regular representation, where multiplicity and dimension separate, eliminated two defective estimators that all regular representation tests had passed. The demonstrated object is a gated estimator; a full parametric matrix model, with the matrices learned, is the next experiment.

</details>


### [9] [A geometry-based deep equilibrium model for image restoration under multiplicative Gamma noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04944)
*Shengkun Yang,Luca Ratti,Zhichang Guo*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A deep learning framework for image restoration that handles multiplicative Gamma noise and blur, using explicit interpretable regularization based on geometric priors (surface area and mean curvature), optimized via mirror descent with proven global convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional deep equilibrium (DEQ) models rely on implicit neural regularization which is difficult to interpret. The authors aim to provide an explicit, interpretable regularizer while maintaining competitive performance.

Method: Learns an explicit regularizer parameterized by geometric priors (surface area and mean curvature) within a variational model. Employs a mirror descent algorithm tailored to Gamma-noise fidelity terms. Convergence established via the Kurdyka-Lojasiewicz property for functions in o-minimal structures.

Result: Outperforms representative model-based approaches on grayscale and color image restoration, and achieves performance comparable to state-of-the-art DEQ models based on implicit regularization while using substantially fewer trainable parameters.

Conclusion: The proposed explicit geometric-prior-based regularization offers a strong alternative to implicit DEQ regularization, providing comparable performance with better interpretability and parameter efficiency.

Abstract: We propose a deep learning framework for image restoration from images degraded by both multiplicative Gamma noise and blur. Unlike conventional deep equilibrium (DEQ) models that rely on implicit neural regularization, the proposed method learns an explicit and interpretable regularizer parameterized by geometric priors associated with surface area and mean curvature. To minimize the resulting variational model, we develop a mirror descent algorithm tailored to the commonly used Gamma-noise fidelity terms. Leveraging the Kurdyka-Lojasiewicz property for functions defined in $o$-minimal structures, we establish the global convergence of the generated iterates to a critical point. Experimental results on both grayscale and color image restoration demonstrate that the proposed method consistently outperforms representative model-based approaches while achieving performance comparable to state-of-the-art DEQ models based on implicit regularization, despite requiring substantially fewer trainable parameters.

</details>


### [10] [A High-Order Perturbation of Envelopes (HOPE) Method for Electromagnetic Scattering by Periodic Inhomogeneous Chiral Media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04950)
*David Nicholls,Liet Vo*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper presents a High-Order Spectral method combined with geometric regular perturbation theory to efficiently and accurately simulate electromagnetic scattering from chiral periodic structures, demonstrating analyticity of the scattered field with respect to chirality deformation and implementing it as a rapid numerical algorithm via High-Order Perturbation of Envelopes (HOPE) recursions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chirality is important in many optical phenomena, and there is a need for efficient and accurate numerical algorithms to simulate solutions in this setting.

Method: The authors couple a High-Order Spectral method with geometric regular perturbation theory, treating the chirality of a laterally periodic, constant permittivity/permeability structure as a deviation from a background value. They use HOPE recursions to establish analyticity results and implement them as a numerical algorithm.

Result: The authors demonstrate analyticity of the scattered field with respect to chirality deformations of arbitrarily large real size and joint analyticity in the spatial variables. The HOPE recursions can be implemented as a rapid, robust, and reliable numerical algorithm.

Conclusion: The proposed method provides an efficient, accurate, and reliable approach for simulating electromagnetic wave scattering from chiral periodic structures, leveraging rigorous analytical guarantees of field analyticity.

Abstract: Chirality plays an important role in many optical phenomena, and it is crucial to have efficient and accurate numerical algorithms to simulate solutions in this setting. In this paper, we discuss a High-Order Spectral method coupled to geometric regular perturbation theory, which results in just such a method. We view the chirality of the laterally periodic, constant permittivity/permeability structure as deviating from a background value, and demonstrate analyticity of the field scattered by a plane electromagnetic wave with respect to this deformation. This analyticity is not only with respect to chirality deformations of arbitrarily large real size, but also joint in the spatial variables. We also show how the High--Order Perturbation of Envelopes (HOPE) recursions, which we used to establish these results, can be implemented as a rapid, robust, and reliable numerical algorithm.

</details>


### [11] [Data generated internal solutions for the plasma wave equation: error bounds and numerical experiments in two dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04989)
*Vladimir Druskin,Shari Moskow,Mikhail Zaslavsky*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper extends the internal solution reconstruction method for time-domain plasma wave equations with unknown potential from 1D to 2D. Using a MIMO setup with block Gramian (mass matrix), the authors show that data-generated internal solutions converge at order √τ, remain accurate for high-contrast composite media, and require eigenvalue-floor regularization (more robust than diagonal shift) when handling noisy response data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors previously showed that in one dimension, data-generated internal solutions for plasma wave equations converge in L² at order √τ using Cholesky decomposition of the data-driven Gramian. This paper extends the theory and numerical validation to two dimensions, where a MIMO setup with block Gramian is required, addressing a more realistic and practically relevant scenario.

Method: The method computes internal solutions by transforming known background snapshots via Cholesky decomposition of the data-driven block Gramian (mass matrix) in a 2D MIMO configuration, with the number of boundary sources increasing as time sampling is refined. The authors prove that the error bound carries over from 1D, then analyze regularization strategies (diagonal shift vs. eigenvalue floor) for noisy data.

Result: Numerical refinement studies on a square domain show relative errors approaching zero at rate √τ and absolute errors going to zero in L¹. Reconstructions remain accurate for high-contrast composite media. For noisy data, the eigenvalue floor regularization outperforms diagonal shift, maintaining better reconstructions than the unperturbed background field with noise up to ten percent of RMS data amplitude.

Conclusion: The 2D extension of the internal solution reconstruction via Cholesky-based Gramian transformation is validated both theoretically and numerically, with √τ convergence confirmed. The eigenvalue floor is identified as a more robust regularization approach than diagonal shift for noisy data in practice.

Abstract: We consider the computation of internal solutions for a time domain plasma wave equation with an unknown potential $q$ from boundary response data. The internal solutions are computed by transforming known background snapshots using the Cholesky decomposition of the data-driven Gramian, or mass matrix. It was recently shown that in one dimension these data generated internal solutions converge in $L^2$ at order $\sqrtτ$ for well chosen initial waves. Here we study the internal solution reconstruction in two dimensions, where a multiple input/multiple output (MIMO) setup and a block Gramian are needed, with the number of boundary sources increasing as the time sampling is refined. We show that the general error bound carries over to this setting: the distance between the data generated solutions and the best causal approximation from background snapshots is controlled by the best approximation mass matrix mismatch. Numerical refinement studies on a square domain measure the convergence of the data generated solutions alongside the best causal approximation from the background snapshots, with the relative errors appearing to go to zero at rate $\sqrtτ$, and the absolute errors going to zero in $L^1$. We also show that the solution reconstructions remain accurate for high contrast composite media. Finally, since the mass matrix assembled from noisy response data can fail to be positive definite, regularization is needed; comparing a diagonal shift with an eigenvalue floor, we find the floor more robust, with reconstructions that remain more accurate than the unperturbed background field with noise that is up to ten percent of the root mean square data amplitude.

</details>


### [12] [A New Error Analysis for a Point Tracking Elliptic Distributed Optimal Control Problem with Pointwise Control Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04281)
*Susanne C. Brenner,Li-yeng Sung*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper presents a unified, self-contained error analysis framework for finite element and variational discretizations of linear-quadratic elliptic optimal control problems with point tracking and pointwise control constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for a concise and unified error analysis covering both standard finite element and variational discretization approaches for elliptic optimal control problems with point tracking and control constraints.

Method: The authors develop a unified error analysis framework that treats standard finite element discretization and variational discretization together, relying only on standard finite element analysis techniques and classical elliptic PDE results.

Result: A concise, self-contained error analysis is established for both discretization schemes within a single framework.

Conclusion: The unified analysis provides a comprehensive error assessment for point-tracking elliptic optimal control problems with control constraints across two discretization approaches.

Abstract: We develop a concise error analysis for a linear-quadratic elliptic distributed optimal control problem with point tracking and pointwise control constraints, where a standard finite element discretization and a variational discretization are treated in a unified framework. The analysis is self-contained modulo standard techniques in finite element analysis and classical results for elliptic partial differential equations.

</details>


### [13] [A Weighted Upwind Vector Kinetic Lattice Boltzmann Method For Hyperbolic Conservation Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04283)
*Michael W. Brown,Jehanzeb Chaudhry,John N. Shadid*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper introduces novel upwinded equilibrium distribution functions for Vector kinetic lattice Boltzmann (VKLB) methods, constructed via continuous flux vector splitting based on eigendecomposition of the flux Jacobian. The method is verified on shallow water, Euler, and ideal MHD equations, demonstrating improved stability, reduced error, and sharper shock resolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VKLB methods have emerged as a promising framework for solving hyperbolic PDE systems, but require careful construction of equilibrium distribution functions. The authors aim to develop a broadly applicable formulation for general hyperbolic systems that improves upon existing VKLB approaches.

Method: The authors construct novel upwinded equilibrium distribution functions from conservation variables and numerical fluxes derived via continuous flux vector splitting based on the eigendecomposition of the flux Jacobian, enabling a weighted-upwind VKLB equilibrium applicable to general hyperbolic systems.

Result: The method is verified on shallow water, Euler, and ideal MHD equations, demonstrating improved stability, reduced error norms, and sharper shock resolution across increasingly complex benchmark problems.

Conclusion: The proposed weighted-upwind VKLB equilibrium formulation offers a broadly applicable and improved approach for solving general hyperbolic systems with enhanced stability and accuracy compared to existing methods.

Abstract: Vector kinetic lattice Boltzmann (VKLB) methods have recently emerged as a promising framework for solving hyperbolic partial differential equation (PDE) systems. VKLB discretizes Boltzmann-type equations using a discrete set of lattice velocities, enforces discrete moment constraints, and carefully defines equilibrium distribution functions. In this work, we introduce novel upwinded equilibrium distribution functions constructed from conservation variables and numerical fluxes derived via continuous flux vector splitting based on the eigendecomposition of the flux Jacobian. This formulation enables the weighted-upwind VKLB equilibrium to be applied broadly to general hyperbolic systems. The method is verified on a set of challenging hyperbolic systems that includes the shallow water, Euler and ideal magnetohydrodynamics (MHD) equations. The proposed method demonstrates improved stability, reduced error norms, and sharper shock resolution across increasingly complex verification and benchmark problems.

</details>


### [14] [Error estimates for vector field interpolation based on generalized matrix-valued kernels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04313)
*Zhengjie Sun,Lishuo Dong,Leevan Ling*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper develops an operator-based framework for constructing divergence-free and curl-free matrix-valued kernels using integral and differential operators, which relaxes the regularity requirements of classical potential-based approaches. The authors prove norm-equivalence of resulting native spaces to vector-valued Sobolev spaces via dimension-walking techniques, derive sharp Sobolev error estimates allowing fractional regularity, establish Bernstein-type inequalities leading to a complete inverse theorem, and prove stability through lower bounds on smallest eigenvalues of interpolation matrices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical potential-based constructions for divergence-free and curl-free matrix-valued kernels naturally enforce structural constraints but require the generating scalar function to have relatively high smoothness, limiting their applicability to data with lower regularity.

Method: The paper introduces an operator-based framework using integral and differential operators to construct div-free and curl-free matrix-valued kernels, applies dimension-walking techniques to prove native space norm-equivalence with vector-valued Sobolev spaces, and derives direct Sobolev error estimates with Bernstein-type inequalities for kernel trial spaces.

Result: The proposed framework substantially relaxes regularity requirements compared to potential-based approaches. The authors obtain sharp direct Sobolev error estimates supporting fractional regularity, a complete inverse theorem via Bernstein inequalities, and stability lower bounds for smallest eigenvalues of interpolation matrices, all verified by numerical experiments.

Conclusion: The operator-based construction provides a more flexible and less restrictive framework for divergence-free and curl-free kernel interpolation, with complete theoretical guarantees including approximation accuracy, inverse theorems, and numerical stability.

Abstract: Matrix-valued kernels provide a flexible framework for approximating vector fields from scattered data, especially when structural constraints such as divergence-free or curl-free conditions must be preserved. Classical potential-based constructions enforce these constraints naturally, but they typically require the generating scalar function to possess relatively high smoothness. We develop an operator-based framework for constructing div-free and curl-free matrix-valued kernels using integral and differential operators, which substantially relaxes the regularity requirements of the potential approach. Using dimension-walking techniques, we show that the resulting native spaces are norm-equivalent to appropriate vector-valued Sobolev spaces. Another main contribution of the paper is a sharp error analysis for the corresponding kernel matrix-valued interpolation problem. We derive direct Sobolev error estimates that allow fractional regularity of the target field, and we establish Bernstein-type inequalities for the associated kernel trial spaces. These results lead to a complete inverse theorem. We also investigate stability by proving lower bounds for the smallest eigenvalues of the interpolation matrices. Numerical experiments are included to verify the theoretical results.

</details>


### [15] [The Refined Joint Bidiagonalization Method and an Implicitly Restarted Algorithm for Large GSVD Computations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04360)
*Kaixiao Fang,Zhongxiao Jia*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the convergence of the joint bidiagonalization (JBD) method for computing extreme generalized singular value decomposition (GSVD) components, showing that Ritz vectors may converge erratically or fail to converge even though Ritz values converge. To fix this, the authors propose a refined JBD (RJBD) method with refined Ritz vectors that provably converge unconditionally, and develop an implicitly restarted variant that is competitive or more efficient than the standard approach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The JBD method is used for computing GSVD components, but its convergence behavior was not well understood. The authors reveal that the Ritz vectors from JBD can be irregular or non-convergent, undermining the reliability of the method for practical applications requiring accurate generalized singular vectors.

Method: The paper provides a convergence analysis showing Ritz values converge while Ritz vectors may fail to converge. It proves an interlacing property between Ritz values and generalized singular values. To overcome the convergence issues, the authors extend the refined Rayleigh-Ritz projection from eigenvalue problems to GSVD, proposing refined right and left Ritz vectors with residual optimality, and extend implicit restarting with refined shifts to the refined JBD method.

Result: The paper proves that refined Ritz vectors from RJBD converge unconditionally under the same hypothesis that caused irregular convergence in the original JBD method. Numerical experiments demonstrate the implicitly restarted RJBD algorithm is at least competitive and often considerably more efficient than the implicitly restarted JBD algorithm.

Conclusion: The refined JBD method successfully addresses the convergence deficiencies of the standard JBD method by ensuring unconditional convergence of the Ritz vectors while maintaining or improving computational efficiency through implicit restarting.

Abstract: We make a convergence analysis on the joint bidiagonalization (JBD) method that computes several extreme generalized singular value decomposition (GSVD) components of a regular matrix pair $\{A,L\}$, and show that the right and left Ritz vectors obtained by it may converge erratically and even may fail to converge, while Ritz values converge. These convergence results hold for a class of general Rayleigh--Ritz projection methods for the GSVD problem under the hypothesis that the deviation of a desired right generalized singular vector from the right subspace tends to zero. We prove the interlacing property of Ritz values and generalized singular values, and extend it to the generalized singular values of $\{A,L\}$ and the matrix pairs consisting of subsets of its columns. To overcome the irregular convergence or possible non-convergence of the JBD method, we nontrivially extend the refined Rayleigh--Ritz projection for the eigenvalue problem to the GSVD problem, and propose a refined JBD (RJBD) method that replaces the right Ritz vectors by new approximations, called the right refined Ritz vectors, satisfying certain residual optimality; we define new approximate left generalized singular vectors, called the left refined Ritz vectors. We prove that the left and right refined Ritz vectors unconditionally converge under the same hypothesis. We extend the implicit restarting scheme to the RJBD method, and develop an implicitly restarted RJBD algorithm with the refined shifts proposed. Numerical experiments illustrate that the new algorithm is at least competitive and often considerably more efficient than the implicitly restarted JBD algorithm.

</details>


### [16] [An adaptive split-combine Gaussian mixture filter for nonlinear and multimodal state estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04430)
*San Kim,Won Chang,Daniel B. Forger,Dae Wook Kim*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper introduces an adaptive split-combine Gaussian mixture filter (AMF) for estimating asymmetric and multimodal probability density functions in nonlinear dynamical systems. The filter adaptively splits and combines Gaussian particles without online numerical optimization, with a splitting method that guarantees variance reduction along a target level-set-point direction. AMF outperforms baseline filters on benchmarks including Van der Pol oscillators and the Lorenz attractor, and a parallel implementation enables practical computational costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Kalman-type filters rely on a single-Gaussian assumption that fails for nonlinear systems with oscillatory or chaotic dynamics, where PDFs become highly asymmetric and multimodal. While Gaussian mixture filtering has been proposed to address this, accurately propagating mixture components and adaptively adjusting their number and weights over time remain unresolved challenges.

Method: The authors develop an adaptive split-combine Gaussian mixture filter (AMF) that estimates time-evolving asymmetric and multimodal PDFs by adaptively splitting and combining Gaussian particles. The splitting method guarantees variance reduction along a target level-set-point direction of a Gaussian particle, enabling accurate and efficient propagation without auxiliary online numerical optimization. A parallel implementation is also proposed.

Result: AMF consistently outperforms various baseline filters across diverse benchmarks, including single and coupled slow-fast Van der Pol oscillators and the Lorenz attractor. The parallel implementation allows high-fidelity PDF estimation with practical computational cost.

Conclusion: The adaptive split-combine Gaussian mixture filter provides an accurate and computationally practical solution for tracking non-Gaussian, asymmetric, and multimodal PDFs in nonlinear dynamical systems, overcoming the limitations of Kalman-type filters in such regimes.

Abstract: Filtering combines model predictions with measurements to estimate the probability density function (PDF) of a system state over time. The PDF often becomes highly asymmetric and even multimodal in nonlinear systems with oscillatory or chaotic dynamics. Such non-Gaussian features violate the single-Gaussian assumption underlying Kalman-type filters. To address this problem, Gaussian mixture filtering has been proposed. However, accurately propagating mixture components and adaptively adjusting their number and weights over time remain open challenges. Here, we develop an adaptive split-combine Gaussian mixture filter (AMF) that estimates the time evolution of asymmetric and multimodal PDFs by adaptively splitting and combining Gaussian particles without auxiliary online numerical optimization. Notably, the proposed splitting method guarantees a reduction in variance along a target level-set-point direction of a Gaussian particle. This enables accurate and efficient propagation of particles. We show that AMF consistently outperforms various baseline filters across diverse benchmarks, including single and coupled slow-fast Van der Pol oscillators and the Lorenz attractor. We also propose a parallel implementation of AMF, allowing high-fidelity PDF estimation with practical computational cost.

</details>


### [17] [An unfitted finite element discrete fracture model for low-permeability barriers via local stiffness matrix modification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04431)
*Ziyao Xu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper proposes a simple extension of the linear finite element method for modeling low-permeability barriers in porous media by modifying the local stiffness matrix of barrier-cut elements with a closed-form expression, while preserving the H1-conforming P1 finite element space, sparsity, and symmetric positive definiteness, plus a local post-processing step to recover sharp pressure jumps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While finite element methods and discrete fracture models are well-established for highly conductive fractures, low-permeability barriers have resisted this framework because they induce pressure discontinuities that continuous finite elements cannot represent directly.

Method: The method modifies the local stiffness matrix of each barrier-cut element via a closed-form expression, retaining the same H1-conforming P1 finite element space without mesh fitting. A purely local post-processing step recovers the discontinuous pressure field with a sharp jump at the barrier interface.

Result: The method preserves sparsity and symmetric positive definiteness, coincides with standard FEM away from barriers, and eliminates the one-element-wide smearing of the continuous solution. Convergence studies with manufactured solutions and 2D/3D benchmark problems confirm effectiveness.

Conclusion: The proposed extension provides an effective, simple, and computationally inexpensive framework for modeling low-permeability barriers with sharp pressure jumps while maintaining the desirable properties of standard finite element methods.

Abstract: Finite element methods are among the most widely used discretizations for flow in porous media, and their discrete fracture models (DFMs) for highly conductive fractures are well-established. Low-permeability barriers, by contrast, have long resisted this framework because they induce pressure discontinuities that continuous elements cannot represent directly. In this paper, we propose a simple extension of the linear finite element method for modeling low-permeability barriers through a closed-form modification of the local stiffness matrix of each barrier-cut element. The method retains exactly the same $H^1$-conforming $P^1$-finite element space, preserves the original sparsity pattern and symmetric positive definiteness, requires no mesh fitting, and coincides with the standard finite element method away from barriers. Moreover, an inexpensive, purely local post-processing step recovers the discontinuous pressure field with a sharp jump at the barrier interface, thereby removing the one-element-wide smearing present in the continuous solution. Convergence studies with manufactured solutions and two- and three-dimensional benchmark problems from the literature confirm the effectiveness of the method.

</details>


### [18] [Optimal convergence of adaptive BEM driven by functional-type error estimators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04556)
*Maximilian Brunner,Alexander Freiszlinger,Dirk Pauly,Dirk Praetorius*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper derives functional upper bounds for the potential error in boundary element discretizations of the Laplace-Dirichlet problem, based on local auxiliary problems defined on patches of boundary vertices. The resulting a posteriori error estimator is shown to be locally equivalent to the residual error estimator, enabling proof of R-linear convergence and optimal convergence rates under adaptive mesh refinement. Numerical experiments confirm the theory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a rigorous functional a posteriori error estimator for boundary element methods applied to the Laplace-Dirichlet problem that can be proven to converge with optimal rates under adaptive refinement, by establishing equivalence with the well-studied residual error estimator.

Method: Derives functional upper bounds for potential error using local auxiliary problems on patches of boundary vertices. Proves local equivalence between the resulting functional error estimator and the residual error estimator, then leverages this equivalence to establish R-linear convergence and optimal convergence rates in combination with an adaptive mesh-refining strategy.

Result: The functional error estimator is locally equivalent to the residual error estimator, which leads to proof of R-linear convergence of the functional estimator. With a suitable adaptive mesh-refinement strategy, both the potential error and the functional error estimator converge with optimal rates with respect to the number of boundary elements. Numerical experiments confirm the theoretical results and demonstrate practical performance.

Conclusion: The proposed functional a posteriori error estimator for the Laplace-Dirichlet boundary element discretization is both theoretically sound (with proven optimal convergence rates) and practically effective, as supported by numerical experiments.

Abstract: In the present work, we derive functional upper bounds for the potential error arising from boundary element discretizations of the Laplace-Dirichlet problem. These bounds are based on local auxiliary problems on patches of boundary vertices and the resulting a posteriori error estimator is shown to be locally equivalent to the well-studied residual error estimator. This equivalence result allows us to prove R-linear convergence of the functional a posteriori error estimator and, together with a suitable mesh-refining strategy, to establish that the potential error as well as the functional error estimator converge with optimal rates with respect to the number of boundary elements. Numerical experiments affirm the theoretical findings and illustrate the practical performance of the related adaptive algorithm driven by the proposed functional error estimator.

</details>


### [19] [A commutant gate for spectral fitting through symmetry forced degeneracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04903)
*Stelios Savva*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Learned spectral models fail at symmetry-forced degenerate sectors due to ill-defined per-level observables in exactly degenerate spaces and singular eigenvector gradients near symmetry-protected crossings. The authors propose a gated estimator that reads symmetry structure directly from observed operators via the linear commutant (using simultaneous block diagonalization), classifying forced versus accidental degeneracies structurally rather than metrically, and switching objectives between projector traces (forced blocks) and per-level targets (elsewhere). The gate classifies correctly down to noise ε≈0.3 (where energy clustering fails at 0.02), achieves machine-precision fitting in symmetric and symmetry-breaking regimes, and eliminates defective estimators via off-regular-representation validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learned spectral models (e.g., neural networks fitting eigenvalue problems) fail precisely where symmetries force degeneracies: per-level observables are ill-defined within exactly degenerate eigenspaces, and eigenvector gradients are singular (proportional to 1/(λ_i−λ_j)) near symmetry-protected crossings. Standard fixes—regularizing the divergence or thresholding the gap—are fundamentally inadequate because a fixed gap cannot simultaneously protect a forced multiplet and distinguish genuinely distinct levels.

Method: The method uses a gate that reads symmetry structure directly from the observed operator family as its linear commutant, computed via one singular value decomposition nullspace following Maehara and Murota's simultaneous block diagonalization. Block identity is read from the centre of the commutant (structural), not from eigenvalue clustering (metric), enabling a distinction between forced and accidental degeneracies. The objective switches between a projector trace for forced blocks and a per-level target elsewhere. Validation is performed off the regular representation where multiplicity and dimension separate.

Result: Under operator estimation noise, the gate classifies correctly to ε≈0.3 while energy clustering already fails by 0.02. Gated fitting reaches machine precision in both symmetric and symmetry-breaking regimes—in the latter converging to a truth that is a singularity of the ungated objective—with observable bias at the noise floor. Off-regular-representation validation eliminated two defective estimators that all regular-representation tests had passed.

Conclusion: The demonstrated gated estimator is a viable fix for symmetry-forced degenerate sectors in learned spectral models, offering a structural rather than metric resolution to the forced-vs-accidental degeneracy problem. The next step is a full parametric matrix model with learned matrices.

Abstract: Learned spectral models fail at symmetry forced degenerate sectors for two distinct reasons. Where symmetry forces levels to coincide exactly, the per level observable one would normally fit is not well defined, since every unit vector of that shared space is an eigenvector; and near a symmetry protected crossing, the eigenvector observable gradient carries a factor 1/(lambda_i - lambda_j) that is genuinely singular as the gap closes. The usual response is to regularize the divergence or threshold the gap, and both carry a real cost: a fixed gap cannot both protect a forced multiplet and keep two genuinely distinct levels apart. We show a different fix, on synthetic operator families with a known symmetry answer key. A gate reads the symmetry structure directly from the observed operators, as the linear commutant of the family, one singular value decomposition nullspace, following the simultaneous block diagonalization of Maehara and Murota. Block identity is read from the centre of that commutant rather than from eigenvalue clustering, which makes the forced versus accidental distinction structural rather than metric, and the objective switches between a projector trace through forced blocks and a per level target elsewhere. Under operator estimation noise the gate classifies correctly to epsilon of about 0.3, where energy clustering already fails by 0.02. Gated fitting reaches the truth at machine precision in both the symmetric and the symmetry breaking regime, in the latter converging to a truth that is a singularity of the ungated objective, and observable bias sits at the noise floor. Validation off the regular representation, where multiplicity and dimension separate, eliminated two defective estimators that all regular representation tests had passed. The demonstrated object is a gated estimator; a full parametric matrix model, with the matrices learned, is the next experiment.

</details>


### [20] [A geometry-based deep equilibrium model for image restoration under multiplicative Gamma noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04944)
*Shengkun Yang,Luca Ratti,Zhichang Guo*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A deep learning framework for image restoration tackling both multiplicative Gamma noise and blur, using an explicit interpretable regularizer based on geometric priors (surface area and mean curvature), optimized via a mirror descent algorithm with provable global convergence, achieving performance comparable to state-of-the-art DEQ models but with far fewer parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional deep equilibrium (DEQ) models rely on implicit neural regularization which is less interpretable. The authors aim to combine the benefits of deep learning with explicit, interpretable regularization for image restoration under challenging combined Gamma noise and blur degradation.

Method: The framework learns an explicit regularizer parameterized by geometric priors (surface area and mean curvature). A mirror descent algorithm is developed tailored to Gamma-noise fidelity terms. Global convergence to a critical point is established via the Kurdyka-Lojasiewicz property for functions in o-minimal structures.

Result: Experiments on grayscale and color image restoration show the method consistently outperforms representative model-based approaches and achieves performance comparable to state-of-the-art DEQ models, while requiring substantially fewer trainable parameters.

Conclusion: Explicit geometric-prior-based regularization combined with deep learning and mirror descent offers an interpretable yet competitive alternative to implicit DEQ regularization for image restoration with Gamma noise and blur.

Abstract: We propose a deep learning framework for image restoration from images degraded by both multiplicative Gamma noise and blur. Unlike conventional deep equilibrium (DEQ) models that rely on implicit neural regularization, the proposed method learns an explicit and interpretable regularizer parameterized by geometric priors associated with surface area and mean curvature. To minimize the resulting variational model, we develop a mirror descent algorithm tailored to the commonly used Gamma-noise fidelity terms. Leveraging the Kurdyka-Lojasiewicz property for functions defined in $o$-minimal structures, we establish the global convergence of the generated iterates to a critical point. Experimental results on both grayscale and color image restoration demonstrate that the proposed method consistently outperforms representative model-based approaches while achieving performance comparable to state-of-the-art DEQ models based on implicit regularization, despite requiring substantially fewer trainable parameters.

</details>


### [21] [A High-Order Perturbation of Envelopes (HOPE) Method for Electromagnetic Scattering by Periodic Inhomogeneous Chiral Media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04950)
*David Nicholls,Liet Vo*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper presents a High-Order Spectral method combined with geometric regular perturbation theory for efficiently and accurately simulating electromagnetic wave scattering in chiral, laterally periodic structures. The method treats chirality as a perturbation from a background value, establishes analyticity of the scattered field with respect to this deformation, and implements the resulting High-Order Perturbation of Envelopes (HOPE) recursions as a rapid, robust numerical algorithm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chirality is important in optical phenomena, and there is a need for efficient and accurate numerical algorithms to simulate solutions in chiral electromagnetic settings.

Method: The paper combines a High-Order Spectral method with geometric regular perturbation theory. Chirality of the laterally periodic structure is treated as a deviation from a background permittivity/permeability value. The authors demonstrate analyticity of the scattered field with respect to both chirality deformations (including arbitrarily large real sizes) and spatial variables jointly, and implement the resulting HOPE recursions as a numerical algorithm.

Result: The method establishes analyticity of the scattered field with respect to chirality deformations of arbitrarily large real size, as well as joint analyticity in spatial variables. The HOPE recursions are shown to yield a rapid, robust, and reliable numerical algorithm.

Conclusion: The proposed High-Order Spectral method coupled with perturbation theory provides an efficient and accurate approach for simulating scattering in chiral periodic structures, with proven analyticity properties and a fast numerical implementation via HOPE recursions.

Abstract: Chirality plays an important role in many optical phenomena, and it is crucial to have efficient and accurate numerical algorithms to simulate solutions in this setting. In this paper, we discuss a High-Order Spectral method coupled to geometric regular perturbation theory, which results in just such a method. We view the chirality of the laterally periodic, constant permittivity/permeability structure as deviating from a background value, and demonstrate analyticity of the field scattered by a plane electromagnetic wave with respect to this deformation. This analyticity is not only with respect to chirality deformations of arbitrarily large real size, but also joint in the spatial variables. We also show how the High--Order Perturbation of Envelopes (HOPE) recursions, which we used to establish these results, can be implemented as a rapid, robust, and reliable numerical algorithm.

</details>


### [22] [Data generated internal solutions for the plasma wave equation: error bounds and numerical experiments in two dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04989)
*Vladimir Druskin,Shari Moskow,Mikhail Zaslavsky*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper extends internal solution reconstruction for a 2D plasma wave equation with unknown potential from boundary data, using a MIMO setup with block Gramian and Cholesky decomposition. Convergence at order √τ is shown, robust to high-contrast composite media, and an eigenvalue floor regularization outperforms diagonal shift under noisy data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend prior one-dimensional results on reconstructing internal solutions of plasma wave equations with unknown potentials from boundary data to the practically relevant two-dimensional setting, where MIMO configurations and block Gramians are required, while addressing numerical convergence and noise robustness.

Method: Internal solutions are computed by transforming known background snapshots via Cholesky decomposition of a data-driven block Gramian (mass matrix) in a MIMO setup with multiple boundary sources. The number of boundary sources increases with refined time sampling. Regularization via eigenvalue floor is compared to diagonal shift for noisy data.

Result: The general error bound carries over to 2D: distance between data-generated solutions and the best causal approximation is controlled by the mass matrix mismatch. Numerical tests on a square domain show relative errors decaying at rate √τ and absolute errors going to zero in L¹. Reconstructions remain accurate for high-contrast composite media, and the eigenvalue floor regularization is more robust than diagonal shift, preserving accuracy even with noise up to 10% of RMS data amplitude.

Conclusion: The 2D internal solution reconstruction method is validated with convergence at order √τ, robustness to high-contrast media, and superior noise handling via eigenvalue floor regularization, establishing it as a viable approach for practical inverse scattering problems.

Abstract: We consider the computation of internal solutions for a time domain plasma wave equation with an unknown potential $q$ from boundary response data. The internal solutions are computed by transforming known background snapshots using the Cholesky decomposition of the data-driven Gramian, or mass matrix. It was recently shown that in one dimension these data generated internal solutions converge in $L^2$ at order $\sqrtτ$ for well chosen initial waves. Here we study the internal solution reconstruction in two dimensions, where a multiple input/multiple output (MIMO) setup and a block Gramian are needed, with the number of boundary sources increasing as the time sampling is refined. We show that the general error bound carries over to this setting: the distance between the data generated solutions and the best causal approximation from background snapshots is controlled by the best approximation mass matrix mismatch. Numerical refinement studies on a square domain measure the convergence of the data generated solutions alongside the best causal approximation from the background snapshots, with the relative errors appearing to go to zero at rate $\sqrtτ$, and the absolute errors going to zero in $L^1$. We also show that the solution reconstructions remain accurate for high contrast composite media. Finally, since the mass matrix assembled from noisy response data can fail to be positive definite, regularization is needed; comparing a diagonal shift with an eigenvalue floor, we find the floor more robust, with reconstructions that remain more accurate than the unperturbed background field with noise that is up to ten percent of the root mean square data amplitude.

</details>


### [23] [A variational framework for Bayesian inversion in moving boundary Darcy flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05317)
*Michael E. Causon,Marco A. Iglesias,Michael V. Tretyakov*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper develops an infinite-dimensional Bayesian framework for recovering spatially varying log-permeability in single-phase Darcy flow with moving boundaries, using an observation-wise representer formulation that enables a reduced Levenberg-Marquardt method for MAP estimation and explicit finite-rank covariance for linearized MAP approximation, achieving dramatic computational savings over MCMC and ensemble methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to address the computational challenges of Bayesian parameter estimation in Darcy flow with moving boundaries (motivated by resin transfer moulding). Traditional approaches like MCMC require 10^5–10^6 forward simulations, making them prohibitively expensive, while existing methods lack efficient formulations that exploit the structure of the moving-boundary problem.

Method: The method introduces an observation-wise representer formulation connecting sensitivities of the parameter-to-observable map to moving-boundary state and adjoint systems. This yields a reduced Levenberg-Marquardt method for MAP estimation on the Cameron-Martin space with explicit finite-rank covariance for linearized MAP approximation. In 1D, closed-form solutions establish posterior well-posedness and Fréchet derivatives. In 2D, a very weak mixed formulation couples pressure, interface velocity, and domain evolution with one-sided directional shape linearization, using independent parallel adjoint problems for representer computation.

Result: Numerical experiments show the 1D LMAP approximation closely matches reference MCMC posteriors while requiring only ~10^1 forward simulations versus 10^5–10^6 for MCMC. In 2D, reconstructions and uncertainty estimates are comparable to ensemble Kalman inversion with large ensembles but at substantially lower computational cost. The workflow generalizes across multiple geometries and experimental configurations.

Conclusion: The observation-wise representer formulation provides an efficient, scalable Bayesian inversion framework for moving-boundary Darcy flow problems. Once representers are computed, all updates and covariance assembly reduce to linear algebra in observation space, independent of discretization dimension, making the method practical for real-world resin transfer moulding applications.

Abstract: We develop an infinite-dimensional Bayesian framework for recovering spatially varying log-permeability from pressure observations in single-phase Darcy flow with a moving boundary, motivated by resin transfer moulding. The main contribution is an observation-wise representer formulation linking sensitivities of the parameter-to-observable map to the moving-boundary state and adjoint systems. This yields a reduced Levenberg--Marquardt method for maximum a posteriori (MAP) estimation on the Cameron--Martin space of a Gaussian prior and an explicit finite-rank covariance for the linearised MAP (LMAP) approximation. Once the representers are computed, the LM update and covariance assembly reduce to linear algebra in observation space, independently of the discretisation dimension.
  In 1D, the explicit moving-boundary solution is used to establish posterior well-posedness and existence of MAP estimators, and to derive closed-form Fréchet derivatives and representers. In 2D, we introduce a very weak mixed formulation coupling pressure, interface velocity and domain evolution. A formal one-sided directional shape linearisation yields the corresponding linearised state and adjoint systems, with observation-wise representers computed through independent, parallel adjoint problems.
  Numerical experiments show that the 1D LMAP approximation closely matches a reference MCMC posterior while requiring \(\mathcal{O}(10^1)\) rather than \(\mathcal{O}(10^5\text{--}10^6)\) forward simulations. In 2D, the method produces reconstructions and uncertainty estimates comparable to ensemble Kalman inversion with large ensembles, at substantially lower computational cost. The workflow is demonstrated across several geometries and experimental configurations without reformulating the inversion methodology.

</details>


### [24] [Structured Tensor Approximation from Lateral Slice Sampling via Basis and Manifold Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05463)
*Jeongmin Chae,Usama Saleem,Selin Bac,Shaama Mallikarjun Sharada,Urbashi Mitra*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: BMTA is a structured tensor approximation algorithm that reconstructs tensors from limited lateral slice observations by combining a quasi-basis model (global smooth variations) with a manifold-guided interpolation model (local slice relationships), using a low-rank Tucker framework. It provides theoretical reconstruction error bounds and demonstrates effectiveness on quantum chemistry and spatiotemporal sensing data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the challenge of tensor approximation when only a limited number of lateral slices are observed, requiring methods that exploit both global and local structural priors of tensors evolving along physical trajectories.

Method: The BMTA algorithm integrates two signal models: (1) a quasi-basis model capturing smooth global variations, and (2) a manifold-guided interpolation model for local slice relationships. A low-rank Tucker decomposition framework is used to efficiently capture these priors, yielding basis function coefficients and a tensor optimization problem. Theoretical analysis provides a non-asymptotic reconstruction error bound.

Result: Numerical experiments on synthetic and real-world datasets (quantum chemistry and spatiotemporal sensing) validate the approach. Theoretical results characterize reconstruction error in terms of sampling complexity, optimization convergence, and model mismatch.

Conclusion: BMTA effectively reconstructs tensors from limited observations by jointly leveraging global basis and local manifold structure, with both theoretical guarantees and practical validation across diverse applications.

Abstract: In this work, we consider a structured tensor approximation problem, where only a limited number of lateral slices are observed. The proposed algorithm , called Basis and Manifold prior Tensor Approximation (BMTA), exploits both global and local structures of the evolution of a global tensor. Specifically, BMTA integrates two signal models: (i) a quasi-basis model that captures smooth global variations along a physical trajectory, and (ii) a manifold-guided interpolation model that characterizes local relationships among tensor slices. A low-rank Tucker reconstruction framework is incorporated to efficiently capture the priors, resulting in coefficients for basis function estimation and a tensor optimization. In addition, we provide a theoretical analysis which establishes a non-asymptotic reconstruction error bound that characterizes the effects of sampling complexity, optimization convergence, and model mismatch. Numerical experiments are performed on both synthetic and real-world datasets, including quantum chemistry and spatiotemporal sensing applications.

</details>


### [25] [A Second-Order Monolithic Scheme for the Coupled Stokes--Biot Model Using Total Pressure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05501)
*Talal Alshehri,Mingchao Cai,Xiaoqin Shen*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A second-order, fully implicit, monolithic BDF2 time-discretization is developed for the coupled time-dependent Stokes and quasi-static Biot system using a three-field total-pressure formulation, with proven discrete energy stability, robustness, and second-order temporal convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
