### [171] [The trace-free Beurling--Ahlfors transform and the Bourgain--Brezis problem for Hodge systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04237)
*Diogo Arsénio*

Main category: math.FA

TL;DR: This paper proves that the dual approach to Bourgain–Brezis estimates for Hodge systems is far more flexible than previously thought. By introducing a trace-free Beurling–Ahlfors transform with a matrix symbol decomposing into odd scalar multipliers, the authors obtain endpoint cancellation estimates enabling the Hilbertian Bourgain–Brezis conjecture in all dimensions and form degrees, along with new Triebel–Lizorkin/Besov estimates and Hodge decompositions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Bourgain–Brezis conjecture concerns sharp estimates for Hodge systems, and prior dual approaches were limited in their flexibility. The authors seek to expand the reach of this method to resolve the conjecture in all dimensions and form degrees.

Method: The paper introduces a trace-free Beurling–Ahlfors transform S on l-forms in R^n, whose matrix symbol decomposes into scalar multipliers odd under orthogonal reflections. This yields an endpoint cancellation estimate from finite measures to L∞ for |D|^{-n}S, enabling completion of the Hilbertian case. The authors also develop multilinear reflection estimates.

Result: They obtain: (1) the Hilbertian Bourgain–Brezis conjecture in every dimension and form degree; (2) new critical Triebel–Lizorkin and Besov Bourgain–Brezis estimates; (3) the Sobolev Bourgain–Brezis conjecture in Ẇ^{n/p,p} for p = 2k/(2k−1), k≥1 (exponents arbitrarily close to 1); (4) endpoint Hodge decompositions and Hodge–Sobolev inequalities; (5) proof that bounded selections cannot be linear, except in the endpoint Besov case where the critical space embeds into L∞.

Conclusion: The dual approach is substantially more powerful than previously recognized, resolving the Hilbertian Bourgain–Brezis conjecture across all dimensions and form degrees and providing new critical Besov/Triebel–Lizorkin estimates, with the caveat that linear bounded selections are generally impossible.

Abstract: We show that the dual approach to Bourgain--Brezis estimates for Hodge systems is substantially more flexible than previously understood. For $1\leq l\leq n-1$, we introduce the trace-free Beurling--Ahlfors transform $S=\frac{n-l}{n}P-\frac lnP^\perp$, a canonical normalization of the generalized Beurling--Ahlfors transform on $l$-forms in $\mathbb{R}^n$. Its matrix symbol decomposes into scalar multipliers that are odd under suitable orthogonal reflections, yielding an endpoint cancellation estimate from finite measures to $L^\infty$ for $|D|^{-n}S$. This cancellation allows us to complete the Hilbertian case of the Bourgain--Brezis conjecture in every dimension and for every form degree. We then develop multilinear reflection estimates and obtain new critical Triebel--Lizorkin and Besov Bourgain--Brezis estimates. In particular, for every dimension and form degree, the Sobolev Bourgain--Brezis conjecture in $\dot W^{\frac np,p}$ holds for $p=\frac{2k}{2k-1}$, $k\geq1$, and hence for exponents arbitrarily close to $1$. We also derive endpoint Hodge decompositions and Hodge--Sobolev inequalities. Finally, except in the endpoint Besov case where the critical space already embeds into $L^\infty$, we prove that the associated bounded selections cannot be linear.

</details>


### [172] [The trace-free Beurling--Ahlfors transform and the Bourgain--Brezis problem for Hodge systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04237)
*Diogo Arsénio*

Main category: math.FA

TL;DR: This paper advances the Bourgain–Brezis conjecture for Hodge systems by introducing a trace-free Beurling–Ahlfors transform with a symbol that decomposes into odd scalar multipliers, enabling endpoint cancellation estimates. This completes the Hilbertian case of the conjecture in all dimensions and form degrees, yields new critical Triebel–Lizorkin and Besov estimates, establishes the Sobolev conjecture for p = 2k/(2k−1), and proves that bounded selections must be nonlinear except in the endpoint Besov case.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the Bourgain–Brezis conjecture for Hodge systems by showing the dual approach is more flexible than previously thought, leveraging a canonical trace-free Beurling–Ahlfors transform whose symbol structure enables endpoint cancellation estimates.

Method: Introduces the trace-free Beurling–Ahlfors transform S on l-forms in R^n, decomposes its matrix symbol into scalar multipliers odd under orthogonal reflections, derives endpoint cancellation estimates from finite measures to L^∞ for |D|^{-n}S, and develops multilinear reflection estimates for critical Triebel–Lizorkin and Besov bounds.

Result: Completes the Hilbertian case of the Bourgain–Brezis conjecture in every dimension and form degree; proves the Sobolev conjecture in Ẇ^{n/p,p} for p = 2k/(2k−1), k≥1 (exponents arbitrarily close to 1); obtains endpoint Hodge decompositions and Hodge–Sobolev inequalities; proves bounded selections are nonlinear except in the endpoint Besov embedding case.

Conclusion: The dual approach with the trace-free Beurling–Ahlfors transform resolves the Bourgain–Brezis conjecture in the Hilbertian setting across all dimensions and form degrees, and yields new critical function-space estimates, while establishing the essential nonlinearity of bounded selections.

Abstract: We show that the dual approach to Bourgain--Brezis estimates for Hodge systems is substantially more flexible than previously understood. For $1\leq l\leq n-1$, we introduce the trace-free Beurling--Ahlfors transform $S=\frac{n-l}{n}P-\frac lnP^\perp$, a canonical normalization of the generalized Beurling--Ahlfors transform on $l$-forms in $\mathbb{R}^n$. Its matrix symbol decomposes into scalar multipliers that are odd under suitable orthogonal reflections, yielding an endpoint cancellation estimate from finite measures to $L^\infty$ for $|D|^{-n}S$. This cancellation allows us to complete the Hilbertian case of the Bourgain--Brezis conjecture in every dimension and for every form degree. We then develop multilinear reflection estimates and obtain new critical Triebel--Lizorkin and Besov Bourgain--Brezis estimates. In particular, for every dimension and form degree, the Sobolev Bourgain--Brezis conjecture in $\dot W^{\frac np,p}$ holds for $p=\frac{2k}{2k-1}$, $k\geq1$, and hence for exponents arbitrarily close to $1$. We also derive endpoint Hodge decompositions and Hodge--Sobolev inequalities. Finally, except in the endpoint Besov case where the critical space already embeds into $L^\infty$, we prove that the associated bounded selections cannot be linear.

</details>


### [173] [A counterexample to the inverse generator problem and related questions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06272)
*Emiel Lorist,Martin Meyries,Mark Veraar*

Main category: math.FA

TL;DR: The paper provides a negative solution to the inverse generator problem on Hilbert spaces by constructing bounded operators with dense range that generate bounded, strongly stable (and even exponentially stable) C0-semigroups, yet whose inverses fail to generate C0-semigroups or have unbounded inverse semigroups with doubly logarithmic growth. This leads to instability of the Crank-Nicolson scheme.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The inverse generator problem asks whether the inverse of a generator of a C0-semigroup also generates a C0-semigroup. The authors aim to settle this question negatively on Hilbert spaces, which has implications for numerical stability of operator approximation schemes.

Method: The counterexamples are constructed from a common finite-dimensional construction using explicit bases of C^{2n} with uniformly bounded partial-sum projections and unconditionality constants comparable to n^α for α∈(0,1). The underlying matrices are obtained as Schauder multipliers with respect to these bases, using eigenvalue sequences with doubly exponentially decaying moduli.

Result: (1) Constructed a bounded operator A with dense range generating a bounded, strongly stable C0-semigroup whose inverse A^{-1} does not generate a C0-semigroup. (2) Constructed an exponentially stable generator with 0∈ρ(A) such that the inverse semigroup is unbounded with at least doubly logarithmic growth. (3) Every Cayley transform of this generator satisfies the ordinary Kreiss resolvent condition but is neither strongly Kreiss bounded nor power bounded, with doubly logarithmic lower bounds on powers. (4) Consequently, the Crank-Nicolson scheme is unstable in operator norm both for fixed step sizes over long times and under mesh refinement at fixed final times.

Conclusion: The inverse generator problem has a negative solution on Hilbert spaces, revealing fundamental limitations of the Crank-Nicolson scheme and Cayley transforms in this setting, even for exponentially stable generators satisfying strong resolvent conditions.

Abstract: We give a negative solution to the inverse generator problem on Hilbert spaces. More precisely, we construct a bounded operator $A$ with dense range on a Hilbert space $H$ that generates a bounded, strongly stable $C_0$-semigroup, while $A^{-1}$ does not generate a $C_0$-semigroup. We also construct an exponentially stable generator $A$ with $0 \in ρ(A)$ such that the inverse semigroup is unbounded and grows at least double logarithmically.
  For the latter generator, every Cayley transform satisfies the ordinary Kreiss resolvent condition but is neither strongly Kreiss bounded nor power bounded. Moreover, its powers satisfy a doubly logarithmic lower bound. Consequently, the Crank--Nicolson scheme is unstable in operator norm both for every fixed step size over long times and under mesh refinement at any fixed final time.
  Our counterexamples are deduced from a common finite-dimensional construction. For $α\in(0,1)$, we use explicit bases of $\mathbb{C}^{2n}$ whose partial-sum projections are uniformly bounded and whose unconditionality constants are comparable to $n^α$. The matrices underlying the counterexamples are then obtained as Schauder multipliers with respect to these bases, using a sequence of eigenvalues whose moduli decay doubly exponentially.

</details>

# quant-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [174] [Leveraging Machine Learning to Gain Insights on Quantum Thermodynamic Entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.06177)
*Srinivasa Rao. P*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A thermodynamic analysis of a quantum engine using a single quantum particle as working fluid, modeled after the classically-chaotic Szilard Map, examining thermodynamic costs of observation and control in quantum vs classical regimes, with machine learning used for energy analysis and simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the thermodynamic costs associated with observing and controlling a quantum particle in a Szilard-type engine, and to shed light on Lindauer's Principle and thermodynamic trade-offs in information-processing-induced dissipation in both quantum and classical regimes.

Method: The engine is modeled after the classically-chaotic Szilard Map with a thermodynamic cycle of measurement, thermal-energy extraction, and memory reset. Machine learning methods are used to perform energy analysis and simulate the quantum engine according to the Second Law of Thermodynamics.

Result: It is demonstrated that energy analysis can be performed and the quantum engine can be simulated according to the Szilard engine-based Second Law of Thermodynamics in working conditions. However, the quantum engine operates via significantly different mechanisms than its classical counterpart, with the cost of inserting partitions playing a critical role in the quantum implementation.

Conclusion: The quantum Szilard engine follows the Second Law of Thermodynamics but operates through fundamentally different mechanisms than the classical version, with partition insertion costs being crucial in the quantum regime.

Abstract: We present a thermodynamic analysis of a quantum engine that uses a single quantum particle as its working fluid, inspired by Szilard's classical single-particle engine. Our design is modeled after the classically-chaotic Szilard Map and involves a thermodynamic cycle of measurement, thermal-energy extraction, and memory reset. Our focus is on investigating the thermodynamic costs associated with observing and controlling the particle and comparing these costs in the quantum and classical limits. Through our study, we aim to shed light on the thermodynamic trade-offs that arise from Lindauer's Principle for information-processing-induced thermodynamic dissipation in both the quantum and classical regimes. Using machine learning methods, we demonstrate that energy analysis can be performed and the quantum engine can be simulated according to the Szilard engine based Second Law of Thermodynamics in its working condition. However, we note that the quantum engine operates using significantly different mechanisms than its classical counterpart, where the cost of inserting partitions plays a critical role in the quantum implementation.

</details>


### [175] [Leveraging Machine Learning to Gain Insights on Quantum Thermodynamic Entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.06177)
*Srinivasa Rao. P*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a thermodynamic analysis of a quantum engine using a single quantum particle as working fluid, modeled after the classically-chaotic Szilard Map. It investigates thermodynamic costs of observing and controlling the particle in quantum vs. classical regimes, using machine learning to simulate the engine and showing it follows the Second Law, though it operates via different mechanisms where partition insertion costs are critical.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the thermodynamic trade-offs arising from Lindauer's Principle for information-processing-induced dissipation, and to compare the costs of measurement and control in quantum versus classical single-particle engines.

Method: Designs a quantum engine following the Szilard Map paradigm with a thermodynamic cycle of measurement, thermal-energy extraction, and memory reset. Uses machine learning methods to simulate the quantum engine and perform energy analysis according to the Second Law of Thermodynamics.

Result: Demonstrates that the quantum engine can be simulated and energy-analyzed via machine learning while satisfying the Second Law. However, the quantum engine operates through fundamentally different mechanisms than the classical version, with the cost of inserting partitions playing a critical role in the quantum implementation.

Conclusion: The quantum Szilard engine follows the Second Law but diverges mechanistically from its classical counterpart, highlighting important thermodynamic trade-offs where partition insertion costs become critical in quantum implementations.

Abstract: We present a thermodynamic analysis of a quantum engine that uses a single quantum particle as its working fluid, inspired by Szilard's classical single-particle engine. Our design is modeled after the classically-chaotic Szilard Map and involves a thermodynamic cycle of measurement, thermal-energy extraction, and memory reset. Our focus is on investigating the thermodynamic costs associated with observing and controlling the particle and comparing these costs in the quantum and classical limits. Through our study, we aim to shed light on the thermodynamic trade-offs that arise from Lindauer's Principle for information-processing-induced thermodynamic dissipation in both the quantum and classical regimes. Using machine learning methods, we demonstrate that energy analysis can be performed and the quantum engine can be simulated according to the Szilard engine based Second Law of Thermodynamics in its working condition. However, we note that the quantum engine operates using significantly different mechanisms than its classical counterpart, where the cost of inserting partitions plays a critical role in the quantum implementation.

</details>

### [176] [Effective Lagrangian regularity and the uniqueness threshold for random Hölder velocity fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05931)
*Maria Colombo,Elias Hess-Childs,Keefer Rowan*

Main category: math.PR

TL;DR: This paper studies ODEs, flow maps, and continuity equations driven by autonomous random velocity fields with multiscale finite range decomposition. The velocity fields are only Hölder regular C^{α-} in space, making the ODE/continuity equation a priori ill-posed. Above the critical threshold α = 1/2, multiscale stochastic cancellations restore well-posedness almost surely away from the zero level set. Below this threshold, examples show robust ill-posedness, marking a genuine phase transition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study addresses a fundamental question: when can one define well-posed dynamics (ODEs, flow maps, continuity equations) for velocity fields that lack classical regularity? Rough velocity fields are ubiquitous in turbulence and stochastic modeling, yet standard well-posedness theory fails in such low regularity settings. The paper aims to understand the precise regularity threshold at which stochastic multiscale structure can compensate for pointwise roughness.

Method: The approach exploits the multiscale finite range decomposition of the random velocity field. The analysis relies on stochastic cancellations across scales to establish well-posedness above the critical threshold α = 1/2. Below the threshold, explicit counterexamples are constructed to demonstrate robust ill-posedness. The authors also derive effective regularity estimates below the critical threshold and extend results to the "refreshing" regime via analogous arguments.

Result: Well-posedness is almost surely restored above α = 1/2 away from the zero level set of the velocity field, despite the lack of pointwise regularity. The threshold α = 1/2 is shown to be sharp: explicit examples below it exhibit robust ill-posedness. Additional effective regularity estimates are provided in the subcritical regime, along with analogous results for the refreshing regime.

Conclusion: The paper establishes a sharp phase transition at α = 1/2 for the well-posedness of ODEs and continuity equations driven by rough random velocity fields with multiscale structure. This provides a rigorous understanding of how stochastic multiscale cancellations can overcome low-regularity obstructions, and delineates the precise regime where such restoration is possible.

Abstract: We study the behavior of the ordinary differential equations, flow maps, and continuity equations associated to autonomous random velocity fields that admit a natural multiscale finite range decomposition. The velocity fields we consider are only Hölder regular in space---$C^{α-}(\mathbb{T}^d)$ for some $α\in (0,1)$---thus the associated ODE and continuity equation are not a priori well-posed. However, above the critical threshold of $α= 1/2$, due to multiscale stochastic cancellations, we prove well-posedness is almost surely restored away from the zero level set of the velocity field. This threshold marks a genuine transition, as demonstrated by examples lying below the threshold that exhibit robust ill-posedness. We additionally provide effective regularity estimates below the critical threshold and prove analogous results in the related "refreshing" regime.

</details>


<div id='hep-ph'></div>

# hep-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [177] [Net electron spin rotation in a plane-wave pulse: Holonomy set by the anomalous magnetic moment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05698)
*N. S. Akintsov,A. P. Nevecheria,S. N. Andreev,Qing-Hua Qin*

Main category: hep-ph

TL;DR: The paper derives the exact spin rotation of a relativistic electron traversing a finite plane-wave laser pulse, showing it reduces to the holonomy of a connection with constant coefficients. The net rotation is an angle proportional to the anomaly a_e squared times the signed area traced by the transverse vector potential—which equals the spin angular momentum (helicity) of the light. The authors verify the area law numerically and analyze how finite focusing restores g-independent contributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To precisely compute the spin rotation (Thomas-BMT effect) for relativistic electrons in finite plane-wave laser pulses, connecting it to the geometric phase/holonomy of a connection, and to understand how the anomalous magnetic moment contributes to polarization changes—particularly isolating the role of the g/2 coupling cancellation on Volkov orbits.

Method: The authors work in an interaction picture built on the exact g=2 evolution, transforming the Thomas-BMT equation into parallel transport with a connection having constant coefficients on the polarization plane. The pulse enters via the closed curve traced by the transverse vector potential. The net rotation is obtained as the holonomy of this connection, yielding an angle of -1/2 a_e² A about the propagation direction. This is compared to two closed-form cases from the 1960s literature and verified numerically over many pulse configurations. Finite focusing effects are analyzed at second order in 1/kw₀.

Result: The spin rotation per pulse is -1/2 a_e² A (holonomy angle), where A is twice the signed area enclosed by the vector-potential curve (the spin angular momentum carried by the pulse per unit area). For a Volkov-orbit electron, the g/2 coupling cancels identically, suppressing the rotation by ~1.3×10⁻⁶ and leaving a channel with no g-independent part. Numerics confirm the cancellation at g=2 over 180 pulse configurations and the area law over 89 more. Finite focusing restores the g-independent part at O(1/kw₀²), exceeding the anomalous signal unless w₀ ≳ 16λ (γ=10) or 270λ (γ=1).

Conclusion: The spin rotation of an electron crossing a laser pulse is a purely geometric effect governed by the area traced by the transverse vector potential, measuring the light's helicity. This provides a clean, exact (at order a_e²) characterization of the spin memory effect, with the anomaly being the only coupling entering in the plane-wave limit; finite focusing can restore larger g-independent contributions under certain beam parameters.

Abstract: We compute the spin rotation that survives after a relativistic electron has crossed a plane-wave laser pulse of finite duration. In the interaction picture built on the exact $g=2$ evolution, the Thomas-Bargmann-Michel-Telegdi equation becomes parallel transport by a connection with constant coefficients on the polarization plane, and the pulse enters only through the closed curve that the transverse vector potential traces there. The net rotation is the holonomy of that connection: an angle $-\frac{1}{2}a_e^2\mathcal{A}$ about the propagation direction, with $a_e=(g-2)/2$ the anomaly and $\mathcal{A}$ twice the signed area enclosed by the curve. That area is the spin angular momentum the pulse carries per unit area, so the rotation measures the helicity of the light. Reduction of the residual dynamics to a rotation coupled through the anomaly alone is an exact result of the 1960s [Ternov, Bagrov, and Klimenko, Sov. Phys. J. 11, 29 (1968); Bagrov and Gitman, The Dirac Equation and its Solutions (De Gruyter, Berlin, 2014), Sec. 5.3], which yields two closed-form cases; the area law is the general second order that those two cases bound. The same area governs the orientation memory of a neutral magnetic dipole [Oblak and Seraj, Phys. Rev. D 109, 044037 (2024)] with a coupling of order unity. For a charged electron on a Volkov orbit the coupling $g/2$ cancels identically, which suppresses the rotation by $1.3\times10^{-6}$ and leaves a channel with no $g$-independent part. We verify the cancellation at $g=2$ over 180 pulse configurations and the area law over 89 more. Finite focusing restores that part at second order in $1/kw_0$, and it exceeds the anomalous signal unless $w_0\gtrsim16λ$ at $γ=10$, or $270λ$ at $γ=1$.

</details>

# cs.SD [[Back]](#toc)

### [178] [Explicit and Stable Pseudospectral Time-Domain Method for the Föppl-von Kármán Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06139)
*Victor Zheleznov,Stefan Bilbao*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: The paper proposes a pseudospectral method for efficient nonlinear modal synthesis of Föppl-von Kármán plates, using spatial-domain grid evaluations for nonlinear products and modal-domain computations for derivatives, with discrete sine/cosine transforms for boundary conditions and SAV time integration for stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Linear modal synthesis is efficient but nonlinear extensions are prohibitively expensive due to the fourth-order coupling tensor required to evaluate products of modal expansions. A computationally tractable approach for nonlinear modal synthesis is needed.

Method: A pseudospectral approach is proposed: nonlinear products are evaluated on a spatial grid (using discrete sine/cosine transforms to switch domains), while spatial derivatives are computed exactly in the modal domain. Simply supported boundary conditions are imposed via the transforms. A scalar auxiliary variable (SAV) technique is used for explicit, stable time integration, with proven non-negativity of the nonlinear potential energy.

Result: The computational cost of nonlinear modal synthesis is significantly reduced while preserving advantages such as precise control over the simulated frequency range. Sound examples are presented.

Conclusion: The pseudospectral method with SAV time integration enables practical and stable nonlinear modal synthesis for Föppl-von Kármán plates at reduced computational cost.

Abstract: Modal synthesis is a widely-used technique for simulation of musical instrument dynamics. In the linear case, a modal decomposition leads to an uncoupled system of damped and forced harmonic oscillators which can be efficiently solved by standard time-stepping methods. However, extensions to nonlinear problems are challenging due to the presence of products of modal expansions in the governing equations. In the case of the Föppl-von Kármán plate, the nonlinear coupling between the modes is described by a fourth-order tensor and is prohibitively expensive to evaluate in the modal domain. In this work, we propose a pseudospectral method in which the products are evaluated on a grid in the spatial domain while spatial derivatives are computed exactly in the modal domain. Discrete sine and cosine transforms between the modal and spatial domains are used to impose simply supported boundary conditions for the plate. Finally, we prove non-negativity of the nonlinear potential energy of the system and employ a scalar auxiliary variable technique for explicit and stable time integration in the modal domain. As a result, we reduce the computational cost of modal synthesis while preserving its advantages like a precise control over the simulated frequency range. Sound examples are presented.

</details>


<div id='astro-ph.IM'></div>

# astro-ph.IM [[Back]](#toc)

### [179] [NestyNet. II. Coherent Function-Space Posteriors from Scientific Neural Surrogates (or How to Avoid Expensive MCMC)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06274)
*Rodrigo Ibata,Wassim Tenachi,Foivos Diakogiannis,Neil Ibata,Anirudh Shankar*

Main category: astro-ph.IM

TL;DR: This paper develops a low-dimensional posterior for scientific neural network surrogates trained with second-order optimization, enabling efficient uncertainty propagation to derived quantities (forces, densities, frequencies) via a linearized measurement-to-function transport map and its leading singular functions (deformation modes).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural networks used in scientific analyses have thousands of correlated parameters, making uncertainty interpretation challenging. There is a need for efficient uncertainty propagation to derived quantities that require derivatives or integrals of the fitted function, without the computational burden of full posterior sampling.

Method: The method linearizes the fitting procedure with respect to randomized residual rows to construct a measurement-to-function transport (linear map) from converged Jacobians and Gauss-Newton curvature. Leading singular functions of this map define coherent deformation modes, and independent Gaussian coefficients generate smooth function draws. Convergence is certified by checking that further optimization steps change predictions by less than a small fraction of measurement errors.

Result: For an 800-parameter phase-space distribution function fit of a mock stellar disk, four uncertainty coordinates (two orders of magnitude fewer than fitted parameters, stable under force basis refinement) capture 99% of vertical-force posterior variance. 4000 coherent draws propagate through force, density, and frequency calculations in 0.8 seconds.

Conclusion: The method provides a highly efficient route to uncertainty propagation for derivative-dependent scientific inference, distinguishing repeated-experiment covariance from local Gauss-Newton/Laplace posterior and conditioning predictions on the fit's declared choices.

Abstract: Scientific analyses increasingly use flexible neural networks, but their thousands of correlated parameters make it challenging to interpret the associated uncertainties. Here we develop a low-dimensional posterior for the fitted function itself, for scientific neural surrogates trained with second-order optimization. Linearizing the fitting procedure with respect to the randomized residual rows gives a measurement-to-function transport, the linear map, assembled from the converged Jacobians and Gauss--Newton curvature, that carries measurement perturbations into the function perturbations that refitting would produce. Its leading singular functions define coherent deformation modes. Independent Gaussian coefficients then generate smooth function draws, so that any derived quantity, including those requiring derivatives or integrals of the draw, inherits the posterior. The construction distinguishes repeated-experiment covariance from the local Gauss--Newton/Laplace posterior and propagates both to correlated quantities of scientific interest. The result is conditional on the fit's declared choices (architecture, hyperparameters, active set, and optimization branch), and every fit is certified as converged by checking that a further optimization step would change the fitted predictions by less than a chosen small fraction of the measurement errors. Our primary example is an 800-parameter phase-space distribution function fit for a mock stellar disk. Four uncertainty coordinates, two orders of magnitude fewer than the fitted parameters and stable under refinement of the force basis, capture 99\% of the vertical-force posterior variance, and 4000 coherent draws propagate through the force, total-density, surface-density, and frequency calculations in 0.8s. The method provides a highly efficient route to uncertainty propagation for derivative-dependent scientific inference.

</details>

# math.SP [[Back]](#toc)

### [180] [Steklov rigidity of Euclidean balls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05577)
*Romain Speciel*

Main category: math.SP

TL;DR: The paper proves a spectral rigidity result: if the Steklov spectrum of a bounded domain Ω (n≥3) with smooth boundary converges to that of a ball at a sufficiently fast rate, then Ω must be a ball. This establishes that Euclidean balls are uniquely determined by their Steklov spectrum among all bounded domains with smooth (possibly disconnected) boundary in any dimension.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A fundamental question in spectral geometry is whether the shape of a domain (e.g., a drum) is determined by its spectrum. For the Steklov eigenvalue problem (which concerns the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map), the uniqueness of balls among all domains is a long-standing open question. The authors aim to resolve this by proving a spectral rigidity result: balls are uniquely spectrally determined in all dimensions, even for domains with disconnected boundaries.

Method: The authors use a spectral rigidity/perturbation approach. They assume the Steklov spectrum of Ω approaches that of a ball at a sufficiently fast rate (quantitative convergence) and then show, through analysis of the spectral problem and its perturbation theory, that Ω must coincide with a ball. This involves a careful quantitative analysis of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator and its spectra.

Result: The main result is that if the Steklov spectrum of Ω tends to that of a ball at a sufficiently fast rate, then Ω is a ball. As a consequence, in any dimension n≥3, Euclidean balls are uniquely determined by their Steklov spectrum among all bounded domains with smooth (and possibly disconnected) boundaries.

Conclusion: The paper resolves the spectral rigidity question for balls in the Steklov setting, showing that balls are spectrally unique among all bounded domains with smooth boundary in any dimension. This strengthens previously known partial results and settles the uniqueness conjecture for the Steklov problem.

Abstract: Let $Ω\subset \mathbb{R}^n$, $n\geq 3$, be a bounded domain with smooth boundary. We show that if the Steklov spectrum of $Ω$ tends to that of a ball at a sufficiently fast rate, then $Ω$ must itself be a ball. In particular, in any dimension and among all bounded domains with smooth and possibly disconnected boundary, Euclidean balls are uniquely determined by their Steklov spectrum.

</details>

# cond-mat.mtrl-sci [[Back]](#toc)

### [181] [A phase field model of coupled crack and dislocations: emission, blunting, and the necessity of dissipative toughening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04781)
*Khanh Chau Le,Thi My Kieu Tran*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A phase field model unifying crack and dislocation evolution from a single energy functional, yielding a closed-form integral nucleation criterion that produces grain-size-dependent yield stress and two-stage fracture/damage response in single crystals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a unified framework where crack and dislocations emerge from a single energy functional, enabling energy-minimization-based predictions of dislocation nucleation and capturing size effects (grain-size-dependent yield stress) that point-wise strength criteria cannot reproduce.

Method: Phase field modeling of a macrocracked single crystal where both crack and geometrically necessary dislocations derive from one energy functional; energy minimization dictates dislocation nucleation via a closed-form integral criterion along slip chords; full non-smooth solver used for coupled computations.

Result: Model reproduces Griffith fracture with slip suppressed; nucleation loads agree with closed-form criterion to 4%; coupled computations show two-stage response: dislocation bands from notch tip lower than cleavage loads blunt/shield the crack raising initiation load, then bands heal as crack grows with a compact like-signed dislocation cluster traveling with the tip; dissipated fracture resistance equals elastic one; in the dissipationless limit emission shields but does not toughen.

Conclusion: The unified energy-based model captures dislocation nucleation with size effects via an integral criterion, reproduces two-stage crack/dislocation interactions, and reveals that toughening requires dissipation (threshold resistance to dislocation motion), to be incorporated in subsequent work.

Abstract: We propose a phase field model of a macrocracked single crystal in which the crack and the geometrically necessary dislocations descend from a single energy functional. Energy minimization alone then decides dislocation nucleation, through an integral criterion evaluated in closed form along slip chords. The criterion yields a size effect inaccessible to point-wise strength conditions: a grain-size-dependent yield stress. With slip suppressed the model reproduces Griffith fracture; with fracture suppressed, the nucleation load measured by the full non-smooth solver agrees with the closed-form nucleation criterion to four percent. The coupled computations produce a two-stage response: at loads an order of magnitude below cleavage, dislocation bands emitted from the notch tip blunt and shield it, raising the initiation load; once the crack grows, the bands heal; a compact cluster of like-signed dislocations travels with the tip, its canceling partner walls pinned at the grain boundary, and the dissipated fracture resistance equals the elastic one. In the purely energetic, dissipationless limit, emission shields the crack but does not toughen it; toughening requires dissipation, incorporated in the sequel through the threshold resistance to dislocation motion.

</details>


### [182] [A phase field model of coupled crack and dislocations: emission, blunting, and the necessity of dissipative toughening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04781)
*Khanh Chau Le,Thi My Kieu Tran*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A phase field model unifies crack and geometrically necessary dislocations in a single energy functional, where energy minimization alone determines dislocation nucleation via a closed-form integral criterion that produces a grain-size-dependent yield stress. The model reproduces Griffith fracture without slip, matches nucleation loads to 4% accuracy, and reveals two-stage coupled response: dislocation bands blunt/shield the crack before growth, then heal during propagation while like-signed dislocations travel with the tip, though toughening requires dissipation beyond the purely energetic limit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a unified framework where crack and dislocation nucleation emerge from a single energy functional, capturing size effects (grain-size-dependent yield stress) that point-wise strength conditions cannot, and to understand the coupled physics of crack growth and dislocation emission.

Method: A phase field model with a single energy functional governing both macrocracks and geometrically necessary dislocations; energy minimization determines dislocation nucleation through a closed-form integral criterion along slip chords. Validation via a non-smooth solver, comparison with Griffith fracture (slip suppressed) and dislocation nucleation (fracture suppressed), plus coupled computations exploring two-stage response.

Result: The nucleation load predicted by the full non-smooth solver agrees with the closed-form criterion to 4%. Coupled computations show two-stage response: dislocation bands blunt and shield the notch tip at loads an order of magnitude below cleavage, raising initiation load; once the crack grows, bands heal with a compact cluster of like-signed dislocations traveling with the tip and partner walls pinned at the grain boundary. Dissipated fracture resistance equals elastic resistance. In the dissipationless limit, emission shields but does not toughen; toughening requires dissipation.

Conclusion: The model demonstrates that energy minimization alone can decide dislocation nucleation through a closed-form criterion, capturing grain-size effects inaccessible to point-wise conditions. Crack-dislocation coupling reveals distinct two-stage behavior, but genuine toughening requires incorporating dissipation through threshold resistance to dislocation motion, which is addressed in subsequent work.

Abstract: We propose a phase field model of a macrocracked single crystal in which the crack and the geometrically necessary dislocations descend from a single energy functional. Energy minimization alone then decides dislocation nucleation, through an integral criterion evaluated in closed form along slip chords. The criterion yields a size effect inaccessible to point-wise strength conditions: a grain-size-dependent yield stress. With slip suppressed the model reproduces Griffith fracture; with fracture suppressed, the nucleation load measured by the full non-smooth solver agrees with the closed-form nucleation criterion to four percent. The coupled computations produce a two-stage response: at loads an order of magnitude below cleavage, dislocation bands emitted from the notch tip blunt and shield it, raising the initiation load; once the crack grows, the bands heal; a compact cluster of like-signed dislocations travels with the tip, its canceling partner walls pinned at the grain boundary, and the dissipated fracture resistance equals the elastic one. In the purely energetic, dissipationless limit, emission shields the crack but does not toughen it; toughening requires dissipation, incorporated in the sequel through the threshold resistance to dislocation motion.

</details>


### [183] [Nitrogen Vacancy Centers in Hexagonal Diamond Exhibit Long Coherence Times](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05320)
*Gabriel Kumar,Siyuan Chen,Victor Wen-zhe Yu,Giulia Galli*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: NV centers in lonsdaleite (hexagonal diamond) are proposed as spin qubits with enhanced coherence. The symmetry-broken AB configuration shows ~4x longer Hahn-echo T2 at zero field due to finite transverse zero-field splitting, while the AA configuration mimics cubic-diamond NV centers. First-principles calculations characterize electronic structure, excitation energies, and photoluminescence fingerprints for experimental identification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NV centers in cubic diamond are leading spin qubits, but their coherence properties are limited. Exploring alternative host materials—specifically the hexagonal diamond polymorph lonsdaleite—could yield NV centers with enhanced spin coherence, beneficial for quantum sensing and quantum information applications.

Method: First-principles calculations examining two NV center configurations (AA with cubic-like symmetry and AB with reduced symmetry) in lonsdaleite, including many-body electronic structure, zero-field splitting, Hahn-echo coherence time estimation, vertical excitation energies, and photoluminescence spectra.

Result: The AB configuration exhibits finite transverse zero-field splitting, yielding approximately fourfold enhancement of T2 coherence time at zero magnetic field. The AA configuration closely reproduces cubic NV center properties. Spectral fingerprints for both configurations are provided for experimental identification.

Conclusion: Symmetry-broken NV centers in lonsdaleite are established as promising candidates for quantum sensing and quantum information science due to enhanced coherence properties.

Abstract: We show that negatively charged nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centers in the hexagonal diamond polymorph lonsdaleite offer a route to spin qubits with enhanced coherence relative to their cubic-diamond counterparts. Using first-principles calculations, we examine two distinct defect configurations, AA, with the same symmetry as in cubic diamond and AB, with reduced symmetry. We find that the AB configuration of the NV center exhibits a finite transverse zero-field splitting, giving rise to an approximate fourfold enhancement of the Hahn-echo coherence time $T_2$ at zero magnetic field. The AA configuration, by contrast, closely reproduces the electronic structure and coherence properties of the cubic NV center. We further characterize the many-body electronic structure, vertical excitation energies, and photoluminescence spectra of both configurations, providing spectral fingerprints for their experimental identification. Our results establish symmetry-broken NV centers in lonsdaleite as promising candidates for quantum sensing and information science applications.

</details>


### [184] [Jsymm: A Python package for symmetry analysis of exchange tensors in magnetic Hamiltonians](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05918)
*A. S. Sergeev,S. V. Streltsov*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Jsymm is a Python package that derives symmetry-compatible exchange tensor forms (Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya and anisotropic Heisenberg exchange) directly from crystallographic data, reducing computational cost and preventing symmetry-violating results in first-principles magnetic calculations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Symmetry constraints determine the allowed forms of interatomic exchange tensors, which govern magnetic phenomena. First-principles calculations of these tensors are computationally demanding, and exploiting symmetry can greatly simplify and speed up these calculations.

Method: Jsymm derives the most general symmetry-compatible exchange tensors from crystallographic data (CIF files). It produces symbolic forms of Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya and anisotropic Heisenberg exchange tensors for any bond, including all symmetry-related bonds. The package offers multiple interfaces: a web interface, interactive text mode, and a Python library.

Result: The package successfully reproduces known symmetry constraints for La2CuO4 and alpha-Fe2O3, and reveals additional relations between exchange tensor components of different bonds. It reduces the number of independent model parameters, dramatically lowering computational cost of ab initio calculations.

Conclusion: Jsymm provides an accessible, user-friendly tool (with web, text, and Python interfaces) that simplifies symmetry analysis of exchange interactions, lowering computational barriers and preventing unphysical results from symmetry violations in magnetic materials modeling.

Abstract: Symmetries of a crystal often restrict its physical properties. In particular, they determine possible forms of the tensors that describe interatomic exchange interaction, which governs a wide range of magnetic phenomena. Computationally demanding first-principles calculations of the exchange tensors can be greatly simplified by taking the symmetry constraints into account. Here, we present Jsymm, a Python package that derives the most general symmetry-compatible form of the exchange tensors directly from the crystallographic data. For any bond formed by magnetic ions, Jsymm produces the tensors of the Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya and anisotropic Heisenberg exchange interaction in symbolic form, as well as the tensors for all other bonds related to it by symmetry. This reduces the number of independent model parameters, dramatically lowering the computational cost of the ab initio calculations and preventing unphysical results arising from symmetry violations. The package accepts standard CIF files and provides a web interface in addition to an interactive text mode and a Python library. We demonstrate its utility on La$_2$CuO$_4$ and $α$-Fe$_2$O$_3$, reproducing known symmetry constraints and revealing additional relations between components of the exchange tensors of different bonds.

</details>


<div id='cs.CE'></div>

# cs.CE [[Back]](#toc)

### [185] [A hybrid s-version isogeometric strategy for dynamic crack propagation in 2D and 3D problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05589)
*Tianyu He,Kosei Kurosaki,Naoki Morita,Naoto Mitsume,Kazuki Shibanuma*

Main category: cs.CE

TL;DR: A hybrid s-version isogeometric analysis (hS-IGA) strategy is proposed for efficient and accurate dynamic crack propagation analysis, combining B-spline global discretization with Lagrange-based local meshes. It removes coupling integrand discontinuities, reduces coupling integration points by 81-95.6% versus the conventional s-method, and accurately evaluates DSIF and local stress in 2D and 3D benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The conventional Lagrange-based s-method suffers from continuity-related bottlenecks in global-local coupling integration that require recursive subdivision. However, a Lagrange-based local mesh is still needed for crack representation and post-processing of fracture quantities like dynamic stress intensity factor (DSIF). This motivates a hybrid formulation that improves coupling integration efficiency while retaining the local mesh flexibility.

Method: The hS-IGA strategy retains the global-local superposition framework of the s-method but uses B-spline basis functions for the global discretization, while preserving a Lagrange-based local mesh in the crack domain. This removes discontinuities in coupling integrands caused by global approximation, allowing standard Gauss quadrature without recursive subdivision for accurate coupling integration.

Result: Verified on 2D stationary and dynamic straight-crack problems (against FEM and conventional s-method) and 3D stationary/dynamic propagating circular-crack problems (against conventional s-method). The strategy accurately evaluates DSIF and local stress, and reduces required integration points by approximately 81% in 2D and 95.6% in 3D dynamic benchmarks relative to the conventional s-method.

Conclusion: The proposed hS-IGA framework provides an accurate and efficient global-local strategy for dynamic crack propagation analyses requiring reliable evaluation of near-crack fracture quantities, substantially improving computational efficiency while maintaining accuracy.

Abstract: A hybrid s-version of isogeometric analysis (hS-IGA) strategy is proposed for accurate and efficient evaluation of near-crack fracture quantities in dynamic crack propagation analysis. The strategy retains the global-local superposition framework of the conventional s-method, while introducing B-spline basis functions only into the global discretisation and preserving a Lagrange-based local mesh in the crack domain. This hybrid formulation is motivated by the continuity-related bottleneck in the global-local coupling integration of the conventional Lagrange-based s-method, and by the need to retain a Lagrange-based local mesh for crack representation and post-processing of the dynamic stress intensity factor (DSIF) and local stress. The resulting formulation removes discontinuities in the coupling integrands caused by the global approximation and enables accurate coupling integration by standard Gauss quadrature without recursive subdivision. The proposed strategy is verified using two-dimensional stationary and dynamic straight-crack problems against the standard finite element method and the conventional s-method, and is further assessed using three-dimensional stationary and dynamically propagating circular-crack problems against the conventional s-method. Results show that the proposed hS-IGA strategy accurately evaluates the DSIF and local stress while retaining the global-local modelling advantages of the s-method. It also substantially reduces the number of integration points required for coupling integration, by approximately 81% in the two-dimensional dynamic benchmark and 95.6% in the three-dimensional dynamic benchmark relative to the conventional s-method. These results demonstrate that the proposed hS-IGA framework provides an accurate and efficient global-local strategy for dynamic crack propagation analyses requiring reliable evaluation of near-crack fracture quantities.

</details>


### [186] [Discrete energy as an exact label-free training objective for finite-element surrogates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05437)
*Ruifeng Cao,Xidan Song*

Main category: cs.CE

TL;DR: This note proves that for linear elastostatics, the assembled discrete potential energy provides an exact label-free training signal for FE surrogate models: the energy gap equals half the squared stiffness-norm error, and the energy gradient equals the stiffness-weighted error, making energy minimisation equivalent to supervised regression in the stiffness norm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training finite-element surrogate models requires reference solutions, which are expensive to compute since each one requires solving the very system the surrogate replaces. The authors seek a training signal that avoids needing reference solutions entirely.

Method: The authors prove identities connecting the discrete potential energy to the stiffness-norm error for linear elastostatics, and add supporting results: a conditioning lemma, a modewise contraction identity, a Chebyshev bound for CG post-processing, and a latent-separation proposition for JEPA pretraining. All numeric claims are implemented as executable falsification checks run on synthetic and real validation data.

Result: All proposed identities and lemmas are proven and verified numerically; the energy gap equals half the squared stiffness-norm error, energy gradient equals stiffness-weighted error, and every inequality passes on both synthetic problems and 16 real validation instances, with measured tightness reported.

Conclusion: Energy minimisation provides an exact label-free surrogate training objective for linear elastostatics, but the construction does not extend directly to elastodynamics via action minimisation; a specific time-discrete formulation is needed to restore exactness there.

Abstract: Supervised training of finite-element (FE) surrogate models requires reference solutions, and each reference solution is obtained by solving the system that the surrogate is intended to replace. The assembled discrete potential energy provides a training signal that requires no reference solution. This note records, with proofs, the identities that make this signal exact for linear elastostatics: the difference between the energy of a prediction and the energy of the reference solution equals one half of the squared stiffness-norm error, and the gradient of the energy equals the stiffness-weighted error. Label-free discrete-energy minimisation and supervised regression in the stiffness norm therefore have the same unique minimiser and identical gradients at every point. Around this central result, the note states a conditioning lemma that bounds the displacement error by the energy gap, a modewise contraction identity that explains why the Euclidean displacement error is an unsuitable primary metric, the Chebyshev bound that governs conjugate-gradient post-processing of surrogate predictions, and a conditional latent-separation proposition for joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA) pretraining on a shared stiffness operator, with an explicit numerical counterexample that delimits its scope. Every claim with numeric content is implemented as an executable falsification check; the checks were executed twice, on synthetic test problems and on a probe set of 16 instances from the validation split of a pre-registered experimental run, and every inequality holds, with the measured tightness reported. A closing section explains why the construction does not extend to elastodynamics through direct minimisation of the action functional, and which time-discrete formulation restores exactness.

</details>

### [187] [The two-particle-irreducible vertex of the two-dimensional lattice $φ^4$ model across the Ising transition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04497)
*Lode Pollet*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The authors reconstruct the two-particle irreducible (2PI) vertex for 2D φ⁴ lattice field theory from Monte Carlo data, tracking it across the Ising transition. The instability is driven by the A₁ (ferromagnetic) channel, with substantial B₁ and B₂ contributions indicating a multidimensional soft sector. The fully irreducible vertex is found to be essentially a local contact, which when inserted into parquet and Schwinger-Dyson equations reproduces Monte Carlo self-energy to better than 0.1% accuracy—benchmarking the dynamical local-vertex approximation (DΓA).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a first-principles benchmark of the dynamical local-vertex approximation (DΓA) by reconstructing the exact 2PI vertex from Monte Carlo data, and to understand the nature of the soft sector and critical behavior in a strongly-correlated lattice field theory.

Method: Monte Carlo measurements of the connected two-particle correlator are used to reconstruct the 2PI vertex Γ(k,p;q). The vertex is decomposed into irreducible representations of the C₄ᵥ point group, eigenvalues of the symmetrized Bethe–Salpeter kernel are analyzed, and the fully irreducible vertex is extracted by stripping crossed-channel ladders. The resulting contact interaction is inserted into parquet and Schwinger–Dyson equations and compared against Monte Carlo self-energy data.

Result: The instability is driven by the A₁ (ferromagnetic) channel at zero transfer with eigenvalue approaching unity. B₁ (nematic) and B₂ (diagonal nematic) channels cooperate across all system sizes, revealing a multidimensional soft sector. The vertex is short-ranged away from criticality but develops a power-law tail at criticality. In the ordered phase, the q=0 eigenvalue collapses as ferromagnetic weight condenses into the order parameter. The fully irreducible vertex is essentially local, and the contact approximation reproduces Monte Carlo self-energy with better than 0.1% accuracy.

Conclusion: The dynamical local-vertex approximation (DΓA) is validated to high accuracy: a local contact fully irreducible vertex suffices to reproduce exact Monte Carlo results. The parquet equations in the critical region behave as a repulsive fixed point driven by a single order-parameter mode, providing insight into the structure of critical fluctuations.

Abstract: We reconstruct the 2PI vertex $Γ(k,p;q)$ from Monte Carlo measurements of the connected two-particle correlator for the two-dimensional single-component $φ^4$ lattice field theory and follow it across the Ising transition. Resolving the vertex in the irreducible representations of the point group $C_{4v}$, we find that the instability is driven by the $A_1$ (ferromagnetic) channel at zero transfer, whose leading eigenvalue of the symmetrized Bethe--Salpeter kernel approaches unity. Substantial $B_1$ (nematic) and $B_2$ (diagonal nematic) contributions cooperate with $A_1$ across all system sizes, highlighting that the soft sector is
  multidimensional. In real space, the vertex is short-ranged away from criticality while it develops a power-law tail at the critical point. In the ordered phase, the $q=0$ eigenvalue collapses because the ferromagnetic weight has condensed into the (one-particle-reducible) order parameter (or collective coordinate for a finite system), although finite-momentum fluctuations persist. By stripping the crossed-channel ladders, we obtain the fully irreducible vertex, which is a local contact -- to a very good approximation. Inserted into the parquet and Schwinger--Dyson equations, this contact reproduces the Monte Carlo self-energy with an accuracy better than one-tenth of a percent. This provides a first-principles benchmark of the dynamical local-vertex approximation (D$Γ$A). Additionally, we demonstrate that in the critical region, the physical solution of the parquet equations behaves as a repulsive fixed point, driven initially by a single order-parameter mode.

</details>


### [188] [Resolving coupled transport in space and time from molecular fluctuations in confined fluids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04920)
*Thê Hoang Ngoc Minh,Ian C. Bourg*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper proposes a space-time-resolved Onsager response matrix framework to describe coupled transport in nanoconfined fluids, extracted from equilibrium molecular dynamics simulations, capturing nonlocal and transient coupling between particle, solute, heat, and charge transport beyond continuum laws.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continuum transport laws parametrized by bulk coefficients become incomplete at the nanoscale, where spatial heterogeneity, molecular structure, and finite relaxation times are intrinsically coupled to transport, requiring a more general framework.

Method: The authors formulate coupled transport in nanoconfined fluids as a space-time-resolved Onsager response matrix and extract it from equilibrium molecular dynamics simulations.

Result: Applied to a confined charged fluid, the framework resolves nonlocal and transient pathways coupling particle, solute, heat, and charge transport. Momentum transport appears as a long-lived nonlocal hydrodynamic mode, while charge transport relaxes rapidly through localized ionic friction. Off-diagonal responses reveal distinct projected dynamics.

Conclusion: The framework provides a microscopic basis for nonlocal, history-dependent transport laws in nanoconfined systems, going beyond continuum descriptions.

Abstract: Transport in fluids is generally reduced to continuum laws parametrized by bulk coefficients and effective interfacial parameters, such as viscosities, diffusivities, slip lengths, and interfacial resistances. This description becomes incomplete at the nanoscale, where spatial heterogeneity, molecular structure, and finite relaxation times are inseparable from the transport process. Here we formulate coupled transport in nanoconfined fluids as a space--time-resolved Onsager response matrix and extract it from equilibrium molecular dynamics simulations. Applied to a confined charged fluid, the framework resolves the nonlocal and transient pathways coupling particle, solute, heat, and charge transport. Momentum transport appears as a long-lived, nonlocal hydrodynamic mode, whereas charge transport relaxes rapidly through localized ionic friction. Off-diagonal responses reveal distinct projected dynamics, providing a microscopic basis for nonlocal, history-dependent transport laws.

</details>


### [189] [The two-particle-irreducible vertex of the two-dimensional lattice $φ^4$ model across the Ising transition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04497)
*Lode Pollet*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper reconstructs the two-particle irreducible (2PI) vertex from Monte Carlo simulations of 2D φ⁴ lattice field theory and analyzes it across the Ising transition. The instability is driven by the A₁ ferromagnetic channel, with substantial B₁/B₂ nematic contributions making the soft sector multidimensional. The fully irreducible vertex is found to be essentially local, and when inserted into parquet/Schwinger-Dyson equations, it reproduces Monte Carlo results with high precision, providing a benchmark for the dynamical local-vertex approximation (DΓA).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the structure of the two-particle vertex across a continuous phase transition (Ising transition in 2D φ⁴ theory) and to benchmark approximation schemes like DΓA against exact numerical results from Monte Carlo simulations.

Method: Monte Carlo measurements of the connected two-particle correlator on a 2D φ⁴ lattice, from which the 2PI vertex is reconstructed. The vertex is decomposed into C₄ᵥ irreducible representations, analyzed via symmetrized Bethe-Salpeter kernels, and the fully irreducible vertex is extracted by stripping crossed-channel ladders. The result is inserted into parquet and Schwinger-Dyson equations to test accuracy.

Result: The A₁ (ferromagnetic) channel drives the instability with its eigenvalue approaching unity at zero transfer. B₁ and B₂ nematic contributions cooperate with A₁ across all system sizes. The vertex is short-ranged away from criticality but develops a power-law tail at criticality. The fully irreducible vertex is essentially a local contact, and using it reproduces the Monte Carlo self-energy to better than 0.1% accuracy. The critical-region parquet solution behaves as a repulsive fixed point driven by a single order-parameter mode.

Conclusion: This work provides a first-principles benchmark of the DΓA, demonstrates that the soft sector near the Ising transition is multidimensional, and establishes that a purely local fully irreducible vertex is sufficient for quantitatively accurate results in this system.

Abstract: We reconstruct the 2PI vertex $Γ(k,p;q)$ from Monte Carlo measurements of the connected two-particle correlator for the two-dimensional single-component $φ^4$ lattice field theory and follow it across the Ising transition. Resolving the vertex in the irreducible representations of the point group $C_{4v}$, we find that the instability is driven by the $A_1$ (ferromagnetic) channel at zero transfer, whose leading eigenvalue of the symmetrized Bethe--Salpeter kernel approaches unity. Substantial $B_1$ (nematic) and $B_2$ (diagonal nematic) contributions cooperate with $A_1$ across all system sizes, highlighting that the soft sector is
  multidimensional. In real space, the vertex is short-ranged away from criticality while it develops a power-law tail at the critical point. In the ordered phase, the $q=0$ eigenvalue collapses because the ferromagnetic weight has condensed into the (one-particle-reducible) order parameter (or collective coordinate for a finite system), although finite-momentum fluctuations persist. By stripping the crossed-channel ladders, we obtain the fully irreducible vertex, which is a local contact -- to a very good approximation. Inserted into the parquet and Schwinger--Dyson equations, this contact reproduces the Monte Carlo self-energy with an accuracy better than one-tenth of a percent. This provides a first-principles benchmark of the dynamical local-vertex approximation (D$Γ$A). Additionally, we demonstrate that in the critical region, the physical solution of the parquet equations behaves as a repulsive fixed point, driven initially by a single order-parameter mode.

</details>


### [190] [Resolving coupled transport in space and time from molecular fluctuations in confined fluids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04920)
*Thê Hoang Ngoc Minh,Ian C. Bourg*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper develops a space-time-resolved Onsager response matrix framework to describe coupled transport in nanoconfined fluids, extracted from equilibrium molecular dynamics simulations. Applied to a confined charged fluid, it reveals nonlocal and transient coupling between particle, solute, heat, and charge transport, showing momentum as a long-lived nonlocal hydrodynamic mode while charge relaxes rapidly via localized ionic friction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional continuum transport laws parametrized by bulk coefficients and interfacial parameters become incomplete at the nanoscale because spatial heterogeneity, molecular structure, and finite relaxation times interact with the transport process. A more complete microscopic description is needed.

Method: The authors formulate coupled transport in nanoconfined fluids as a space-time-resolved Onsager response matrix and extract it from equilibrium molecular dynamics simulations.

Result: Applied to a confined charged fluid, the framework resolves nonlocal and transient pathways coupling particle, solute, heat, and charge transport. Momentum transport appears as a long-lived nonlocal hydrodynamic mode, while charge transport relaxes rapidly through localized ionic friction. Off-diagonal responses reveal distinct projected dynamics.

Conclusion: The framework provides a microscopic basis for nonlocal, history-dependent transport laws in nanoconfined fluids, going beyond continuum descriptions.

Abstract: Transport in fluids is generally reduced to continuum laws parametrized by bulk coefficients and effective interfacial parameters, such as viscosities, diffusivities, slip lengths, and interfacial resistances. This description becomes incomplete at the nanoscale, where spatial heterogeneity, molecular structure, and finite relaxation times are inseparable from the transport process. Here we formulate coupled transport in nanoconfined fluids as a space--time-resolved Onsager response matrix and extract it from equilibrium molecular dynamics simulations. Applied to a confined charged fluid, the framework resolves the nonlocal and transient pathways coupling particle, solute, heat, and charge transport. Momentum transport appears as a long-lived, nonlocal hydrodynamic mode, whereas charge transport relaxes rapidly through localized ionic friction. Off-diagonal responses reveal distinct projected dynamics, providing a microscopic basis for nonlocal, history-dependent transport laws.

</details>
