### [191] [Unscented KalmanNet: a hybrid deep learning filter with calibrated posterior covariance for nonlinear state estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04201)
*Minhyeok Ko,Abdollah Shafieezadeh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The Unscented KalmanNet (UKN) augments the UKF with two learned components—NoiseNet for bounded covariance correction and GainNet for bounded gain residual correction—preserving explicit sigma-point covariance recursion while jointly optimizing accuracy and calibration. It outperforms UKF, KalmanNet, and Bayesian KalmanNet on synthetic and real-flight datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional UKF suffers from unknown, time-varying noise statistics and model mismatch that degrade both estimation accuracy and covariance calibration. Existing learned filters improve accuracy but are built on the extended Kalman filter and either omit explicit covariance or fail to correct mismatch-induced gain bias.

Method: UKN augments the UKF with two structurally distinct learned components: NoiseNet predicts time-varying process/measurement covariances as bounded multiplicative corrections to fixed baselines (guaranteeing positive definiteness), and GainNet applies bounded residual corrections to the analytical gain. A calibration-aware training objective combines state error with covariance- and innovation-consistency terms through adaptive weights.

Result: UKN achieves lowest aggregate state-estimation error across all four test cases (3 synthetic systems + UZH-FPV real flight data), reduces RMSE by 26.4-49.7% vs UKF on synthetic cases, 22.4% and 34.3% reductions in mean position and velocity RMSE over 11 flights, lowest fold-to-fold variability, and normalized NEES/empirical coverage closest to nominal.

Conclusion: UKN demonstrates that augmenting the UKF with learned covariance and gain corrections provides a robust hybrid estimator that simultaneously improves accuracy and uncertainty calibration, addressing the key limitations of both classical UKF and prior learned filters.

Abstract: State estimation for nonlinear dynamical systems is commonly performed with the Unscented Kalman filter (UKF), which propagates the state moments through deterministic sigma points and reports a posterior covariance at every step. In practice, however, unknown and time-varying noise statistics and model mismatch degrade both estimation accuracy and covariance calibration. Existing learned filters improve accuracy but are largely built on the extended Kalman filter and either forgo an explicit covariance or learn uncertainty without correcting mismatch-induced gain bias. This paper introduces the Unscented KalmanNet (UKN), a hybrid recursive estimator that augments the UKF with two structurally distinct learned components while preserving its explicit sigma-point covariance recursion. NoiseNet predicts time-varying process and measurement covariances as bounded multiplicative corrections to fixed baselines, guaranteeing positive definiteness, while GainNet applies a bounded residual correction to the analytical gain. A calibration-aware training objective combines state error with covariance- and innovation-consistency terms through adaptive weights, jointly optimizing accuracy and calibration. UKN is benchmarked against UKF, KalmanNet, and Bayesian KalmanNet on three synthetic systems and UZH-FPV real-flight data. It achieves the lowest aggregate state-estimation error in all four examples and reduces RMSE by $26.4$-$49.7\%$ compared to UKF in the synthetic cases. Leave-one-sequence-out cross-validation over 11 flights shows $22.4\%$ and $34.3\%$ reductions in mean position and velocity RMSE, respectively. UKN also yields the lowest fold-to-fold variability, with normalized NEES and empirical coverage closest to nominal values among the other filters.

</details>


### [192] [Physics-informed reduced-order modelling with equivariant spectral submanifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04239)
*Georg Maierhofer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces equivariant spectral submanifold (eSSM) reduction, which incorporates symmetries of the full-order model into SSM-based nonlinear reduced-order modeling. The authors prove SSMs are naturally equivariant submanifolds and develop an equivariant SSM reduction algorithm that achieves substantially faster computations and improved robustness, demonstrated on benchmark problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While SSM reduction provides mathematically principled nonlinear reduced-order models that go beyond linear techniques like DMD, the computation of SSMs remains computationally expensive, especially for high-dimensional systems. This motivates finding a way to accelerate SSM computation.

Method: The authors (1) establish mathematical foundations by proving that SSMs are naturally equivariant submanifolds and that associated charts and reduced dynamics inherit induced group actions, and (2) exploit these symmetry properties to develop a novel equivariant SSM reduction algorithm that reduces computational cost while improving model robustness.

Result: The equivariant SSM reduction algorithm achieves substantially faster computations and improved model robustness compared to standard SSM reduction, demonstrated on several benchmark problems including a test from the Common Task Framework for Science.

Conclusion: Incorporating symmetries into SSM reduction through equivariance provides a principled way to significantly accelerate nonlinear reduced-order model computation while enhancing robustness, validating the mathematical and algorithmic framework introduced.

Abstract: Spectral submanifold (SSM) reduction has emerged as a mathematically principled route to reliable nonlinear reduced-order models, capturing dynamics beyond the reach of linear techniques such as Dynamic Mode Decomposition (DMD). The computation of SSMs, however, remains computationally expensive, particularly for high-dimensional systems. In this work, we introduce equivariant spectral submanifold (eSSM) reduction, a novel extension of the SSM framework that explicitly incorporates symmetries of the full-order model into the reduction process. We establish the mathematical foundations of this approach by showing that SSMs are naturally equivariant submanifolds and that the associated charts and reduced dynamics inherit the appropriate induced group actions. Building on this framework, we develop a novel equivariant SSM reduction algorithm that exploits these symmetries to achieve substantially faster computations while also improving model robustness. We demonstrate the advantages of this approach on several benchmark problems including a test from the Common Task Framework for Science.

</details>


### [193] [Unscented KalmanNet: a hybrid deep learning filter with calibrated posterior covariance for nonlinear state estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04201)
*Minhyeok Ko,Abdollah Shafieezadeh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces Unscented KalmanNet (UKN), a hybrid recursive estimator that augments the UKF with two learned components: NoiseNet predicts time-varying process and measurement covariances as bounded multiplicative corrections, while GainNet applies a bounded residual correction to the analytical gain. A calibration-aware training objective jointly optimizes accuracy and covariance calibration. UKN achieves the lowest state-estimation error across four benchmarks and improves RMSE by 26.4-49.7% over UKF on synthetic systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard UKF suffers from degradation in both estimation accuracy and covariance calibration when faced with unknown/time-varying noise statistics and model mismatch. Existing learned filters built on EKF either lack explicit covariance estimation or learn uncertainty without correcting the bias-induced gain errors, leaving a gap for a hybrid approach that combines UKF's principled sigma-point recursion with learned corrections.

Method: UKN augments the UKF with two structurally distinct learned components while preserving its explicit sigma-point covariance recursion. NoiseNet predicts time-varying process and measurement covariances as bounded multiplicative corrections to fixed baselines, guaranteeing positive definiteness. GainNet applies a bounded residual correction to the analytical gain. A calibration-aware training objective combines state error with covariance- and innovation-consistency terms through adaptive weights.

Result: UKN achieves the lowest aggregate state-estimation error across three synthetic systems and UZH-FPV real-flight data. It reduces RMSE by 26.4-49.7% compared to UKF on synthetic cases, and shows 22.4% and 34.3% reductions in mean position and velocity RMSE respectively over 11 flights. UKN also yields the lowest fold-to-fold variability with normalized NEES and empirical coverage closest to nominal values.

Conclusion: UKN demonstrates that augmenting the UKF with learned noise and gain corrections, combined with calibration-aware training, simultaneously improves both estimation accuracy and covariance calibration, outperforming UKF, KalmanNet, and Bayesian KalmanNet across all benchmarks.

Abstract: State estimation for nonlinear dynamical systems is commonly performed with the Unscented Kalman filter (UKF), which propagates the state moments through deterministic sigma points and reports a posterior covariance at every step. In practice, however, unknown and time-varying noise statistics and model mismatch degrade both estimation accuracy and covariance calibration. Existing learned filters improve accuracy but are largely built on the extended Kalman filter and either forgo an explicit covariance or learn uncertainty without correcting mismatch-induced gain bias. This paper introduces the Unscented KalmanNet (UKN), a hybrid recursive estimator that augments the UKF with two structurally distinct learned components while preserving its explicit sigma-point covariance recursion. NoiseNet predicts time-varying process and measurement covariances as bounded multiplicative corrections to fixed baselines, guaranteeing positive definiteness, while GainNet applies a bounded residual correction to the analytical gain. A calibration-aware training objective combines state error with covariance- and innovation-consistency terms through adaptive weights, jointly optimizing accuracy and calibration. UKN is benchmarked against UKF, KalmanNet, and Bayesian KalmanNet on three synthetic systems and UZH-FPV real-flight data. It achieves the lowest aggregate state-estimation error in all four examples and reduces RMSE by $26.4$-$49.7\%$ compared to UKF in the synthetic cases. Leave-one-sequence-out cross-validation over 11 flights shows $22.4\%$ and $34.3\%$ reductions in mean position and velocity RMSE, respectively. UKN also yields the lowest fold-to-fold variability, with normalized NEES and empirical coverage closest to nominal values among the other filters.

</details>


### [194] [Physics-informed reduced-order modelling with equivariant spectral submanifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04239)
*Georg Maierhofer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces equivariant spectral submanifold (eSSM) reduction, an extension of SSM-based nonlinear model reduction that explicitly incorporates symmetries of the full-order model. The authors prove that SSMs are naturally equivariant submanifolds, develop a symmetry-exploiting reduction algorithm, and demonstrate substantial computational speedups and improved robustness on benchmark problems including a Common Task Framework for Science test.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While SSM reduction provides principled nonlinear reduced-order models superior to linear methods like DMD, computing SSMs remains computationally expensive, especially for high-dimensional systems. The paper aims to address this computational bottleneck by exploiting system symmetries.

Method: The authors extend the SSM framework by incorporating symmetries of the full-order model. They establish mathematical foundations showing SSMs are equivariant submanifolds with charts and reduced dynamics inheriting induced group actions, then develop an equivariant SSM reduction algorithm that exploits these symmetries for faster computation.

Result: The equivariant approach achieves substantially faster computations while improving model robustness, demonstrated on several benchmark problems including a test from the Common Task Framework for Science.

Conclusion: Equivariant SSM reduction successfully leverages symmetry structure to make SSM-based nonlinear model reduction substantially more efficient and robust, broadening its applicability to high-dimensional systems.

Abstract: Spectral submanifold (SSM) reduction has emerged as a mathematically principled route to reliable nonlinear reduced-order models, capturing dynamics beyond the reach of linear techniques such as Dynamic Mode Decomposition (DMD). The computation of SSMs, however, remains computationally expensive, particularly for high-dimensional systems. In this work, we introduce equivariant spectral submanifold (eSSM) reduction, a novel extension of the SSM framework that explicitly incorporates symmetries of the full-order model into the reduction process. We establish the mathematical foundations of this approach by showing that SSMs are naturally equivariant submanifolds and that the associated charts and reduced dynamics inherit the appropriate induced group actions. Building on this framework, we develop a novel equivariant SSM reduction algorithm that exploits these symmetries to achieve substantially faster computations while also improving model robustness. We demonstrate the advantages of this approach on several benchmark problems including a test from the Common Task Framework for Science.

</details>


### [195] [Potential Matching Optimal Transport: Continuous Normalizing Flows for Exact $p$-Wasserstein Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05666)
*Lishuo Zhang,Ruizhi Huang,Yang Yu,Lei Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PMOT is a potential-flow framework for general p-cost optimal transport that parameterizes the CNF velocity field with a scalar potential in generalized Benamou-Brenier form, trains via self-induced matching loss, and achieves zero-loss exactness recovering p-optimal transport maps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a framework that handles general p-cost optimal transport (c_p(x,y)=||x-y||^p) with flexible terminal distribution matching, beyond the standard 2-cost OT setting.

Method: PMOT parameterizes the CNF velocity field using a scalar potential in the generalized Benamou-Brenier form for the chosen exponent p, and trains the potential gradient with a self-induced matching loss along straight bridges determined by the model's own endpoints, while allowing flexible terminal distribution matching.

Result: Zero-loss exactness is established: any zero-loss solution satisfies the generalized Benamou-Brenier optimality system and recovers the p-optimal transport map and dynamics. On synthetic benchmarks PMOT learns p-specific maps agreeing with p-matched OT references; competitive as likelihood-based density model on high-dimensional tabular data; MMD-based color transformation demonstrates flexible sample-based terminal matching.

Conclusion: PMOT provides a principled potential-flow approach to general p-cost OT with theoretical guarantees on optimality and practical competitive performance across density modeling and sample-based terminal matching tasks.

Abstract: We introduce Potential Matching Optimal Transport (PMOT), a potential-flow framework for general $p$-cost optimal transport with $c_p(x,y)=\|x-y\|^p$. PMOT parameterizes the CNF velocity field with a scalar potential in the generalized Benamou--Brenier form for the chosen exponent $p$. It trains the potential gradient with a self-induced matching loss along straight bridges determined by the model's own endpoints, while allowing flexible terminal distribution matching. Our main result establishes zero-loss exactness: under the stated regularity, exact terminal matching, and uniqueness assumptions, any zero-loss solution satisfies the generalized Benamou--Brenier optimality system and recovers the corresponding $p$-optimal transport map and dynamics. On synthetic benchmarks, PMOT learns $p$-specific maps that agree with the corresponding $p$-matched OT references. It also remains competitive as a likelihood-based density model on high-dimensional tabular data, and an MMD-based color transformation experiment demonstrates flexible sample-based terminal matching.

</details>


### [196] [Alternating Levenberg-Marquardt Training of Physics-Informed Neural Networks with Fourier-Enhanced Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05892)
*Yulun Wu,Matthieu Barreau,Miguel Aguiar,Karl H. Johansson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FALM-PINN is an optimization framework for physics-informed neural networks that decouples representation learning from coefficient fitting using a bilevel optimization scheme with Fourier-enhanced bases and Levenberg-Marquardt algorithm, achieving up to two orders of magnitude lower errors on high-frequency and nonlinear PDEs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PINNs struggle with high-frequency/multi-scale solutions and strongly nonlinear PDEs due to spectral bias (underfitting high-frequency features) and representation-coefficient coupling (entanglement of representation learning and coefficient fitting in a single nonconvex objective).

Method: The method uses a bilevel optimization framework: the upper-level problem learns a Fourier-enhanced basis to enrich the latent space with high-frequency components, while the lower-level problem fits projection coefficients on this basis using the Levenberg-Marquardt algorithm for nonlinear least-squares. For linear PDEs, it reduces to a single-step convex optimization. Global convergence of the alternating training is proven.

Result: Numerical experiments on challenging high-frequency and nonlinear PDEs show FALM-PINN achieves relative L2 errors up to two orders of magnitude lower than state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: FALM-PINN effectively addresses spectral bias and representation-coefficient coupling in PINNs, providing a general framework applicable to nonlinear and coupled PDE systems with proven convergence guarantees and significantly improved accuracy.

Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) often fail to accurately resolve partial differential equations (PDEs) with high-frequency or multi-scale solutions, as well as strongly nonlinear problems. Two factors underlie this difficulty: spectral bias, the tendency of neural networks to underfit high-frequency features; and representation-coefficient coupling, the entanglement of representation learning and coefficient fitting within a single nonconvex optimization objective. In this work, we propose the Fourier-enhanced alternating Levenberg--Marquardt PINN (FALM-PINN), an optimization framework that decouples representation learning from coefficient fitting. The upper-level problem learns a Fourier-enhanced basis that enriches the latent space with high-frequency components, while the lower-level problem resolves the coupling by fitting the projection coefficients on this basis, solving a nonlinear least-squares problem with the Levenberg--Marquardt algorithm. The framework applies to general nonlinear and coupled PDE systems, and reduces to a single-step convex optimization problem for linear PDEs. We prove global convergence of the alternating training scheme in both cases. Numerical examples on multiple challenging high-frequency and nonlinear PDEs show that FALM-PINN achieves relative $L^2$ errors up to two orders of magnitude lower than state-of-the-art baselines.

</details>


### [197] [Kastor: An efficient fine-tuning strategy for generative emulation of PDE simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06107)
*Guillaume Couairon,Alexis Jacq,Yu-Han Wu,Renu Singh,Yana Hasson,Quentin Berthet,Romuald Elie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Kastor is a methodology for converting deterministic physics foundation models into efficient generative surrogates for PDE simulations, combining a two-stage inference scheme (causal auto-regression + temporal super-resolution), Mean prediction regularization (MPR) training objective, and spatial gradient matching. It outperforms competing methods on The Well benchmark, achieving 42.9% average forecasting error reduction vs. Walrus finetuning and beating Walrus on 8/10 datasets in VRMSE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard auto-regressive ML emulators for physical simulations suffer from error accumulation over long horizons and struggle to capture stochasticity of complex physical systems, limiting their practical utility as replacements for traditional PDE solvers.

Method: (1) A two-stage inference scheme combining a large-stride causal auto-regressive model with a non-causal temporal super-resolution network to reduce error accumulation and computational cost; (2) Mean prediction regularization (MPR), a novel training objective constraining the generative model to predict the deterministic distribution mean under null noise conditioning; (3) Spatial gradient matching to improve accuracy and physical fidelity measured via power spectrum density.

Result: On The Well benchmark, Kastor achieves a 42.9% average reduction in forecasting error compared to a reference based on Walrus finetuning methodology, and outperforms Walrus on 8 out of 10 datasets in variance-normalized RMSE (VRMSE), along with better spectral consistency and computational efficiency.

Conclusion: Combining the proposed components—two-stage inference, MPR regularization, and spatial gradient matching—yields a generative surrogate that is both highly accurate and computationally efficient, effectively addressing error accumulation and stochasticity in ML-based physical simulation.

Abstract: Machine learning offers a promising avenue to accelerate physical simulations by replacing computationally expensive traditional Partial Differential Equation (PDE) solvers with fast, differentiable surrogate models. However, standard auto-regressive ML emulators often suffer from error accumulation over long horizons and struggle to capture the stochasticity of complex physical systems. In this paper, we propose Kastor, a comprehensive methodology to adapt a deterministic physics foundation model into a highly efficient and accurate generative surrogate. First, we introduce a two-stage inference scheme that combines a large-stride causal auto-regressive model with a non-causal temporal super-resolution network, significantly reducing error accumulation while minimizing computational cost. Second, we present Mean prediction regularization (MPR), a novel training objective that constrains the generative model to predict the deterministic distribution mean under null noise conditioning. This regularization dramatically improves the performance and stability of both Functional Generative Networks (FGN) and diffusion-based emulators. Finally, we demonstrate that incorporating spatial gradient matching improves the accuracy and physical fidelity of the simulations as measured by power spectrum density. Extensive evaluations on diverse simulation datasets of the benchmark The Well show that with these components, our model outperforms competing methods in forecasting accuracy, spectral consistency, and computational efficiency. Our model achieves a 42.9% average reduction in forecasting compared to our reference based on the Walrus finetuning methodology, and outperforms Walrus for 8 out of 10 datasets on variance-normalized RMSE (VRMSE).

</details>

### [198] [Transparent boundary conditions for the spatially discrete Schrödinger equation: Reflectionless quantum transport in 1D lattices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05338)
*Mashrab E. Akramov,Jambul R. Yusupov,Matthias Ehrhardt,Davron U. Matrasulov*

Main category: math-ph

TL;DR: This paper derives exact transparent boundary conditions (TBCs) for a time-continuous, spatially discrete Schrödinger equation on a 1D quantum lattice, using Laplace transforms and Bessel functions, with rigorous proof of consistency with the continuous case and an efficient trapezoidal-rule time discretization verified by Crank-Nicolson numerical experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To eliminate spurious backscattering at computational domain boundaries in simulations of quantum lattice systems, requiring exact boundary conditions that are transparent to outgoing waves.

Method: The authors use a recently developed exact solution for the discrete Schrödinger system to derive analytic Dirichlet-to-Neumann maps via Laplace transforms, yielding convolution-type boundary conditions expressed through Bessel functions. They prove consistency with the continuous counterpart in the continuum limit and implement an efficient time-discretization using the trapezoidal rule.

Result: Numerical experiments with a Crank-Nicolson solver demonstrate that the proposed TBCs completely eliminate spurious backscattering and preserve reflectionless propagation of Gaussian wave packets exiting the computational domain.

Conclusion: Exact TBCs for the discrete Schrödinger equation on a lattice are achievable and practical, providing reflectionless boundary treatment with rigorous theoretical grounding and efficient numerical implementation.

Abstract: We construct exact transparent boundary conditions (TBCs) for a time-continuous, spatially discrete Schrödinger equation that models a one-dimensional quantum lattice. Using a recently developed exact solution for the discrete system, we derive the Dirichlet-to-Neumann maps analytically via Laplace transforms. This yields a convolution-type boundary condition governed by Bessel functions. We rigorously demonstrate the consistency of this discrete formulation with its continuous counterpart in the continuum limit. Additionally, we present an efficient time-discretization scheme based on the trapezoidal rule for practical implementation. Numerical experiments using a Crank-Nicolson solver verify that our proposed TBCs eliminate spurious backscattering entirely and preserve reflectionless propagation of a Gaussian wave packet exiting the computational domain

</details>
